attraction signal as the resource-weighted sum of member containers' own
genuine, calm operational output:

Attract(M,t) = Σ\_\{i∈M\} R\_i(t)·(1 − s\_frag(i,t))·e\_i(t),

reusing each member's resource scale R\_i, structural fragmentation
s\_frag (low fragmentation contributing more, consistent with a
genuinely integrated cluster signaling more clearly than a
loosely-associated one), and usable adaptive energy e\_i --- a real,
observable quantity growing directly with the size, health, and cohesion
of the matrix, not an assumed constant.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.2.17.1 (Competing Attraction).}} For a
vulnerable container j within range of both a predatory candidate k
(predatory pull 𝒱\_\{k→j\}(t), §8.6a.4) and a horizontal matrix M
(Attract(M,t) above), the real coupling outcome is probabilistic,
favoring whichever signal dominates:

P(j couples to M rather than k) = Attract(M,t) / (Attract(M,t) +
κ\_pred·𝒱\_\{k→j\}(t)), κ\_pred \textgreater{} 0 fitted,

recovering near-certain predatory capture when Attract(M,t) → 0 (no
visible healthy alternative nearby, the ordinary case §8.6a.4 already
covers) and near-certain matrix affiliation when Attract(M,t) dominates
--- the formal content of a large, visibly thriving horizontal matrix
genuinely outcompeting predatory pull for vulnerable containers in its
vicinity, rather than merely coexisting with it. This is a real,
falsifiable prediction distinct from anything predatory targeting alone
produces: matched-vulnerability containers near a strong, visible
horizontal matrix should show measurably lower predatory-capture rates
than otherwise-identical containers with no such matrix nearby.

\hypertarget{b.2.18-repetition-compulsion-drive}{%
\subsection{10.5b.2.18 Repetition-Compulsion
Drive}\label{b.2.18-repetition-compulsion-drive}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.2.18 (Repetition-Compulsion Drive).}} For
container i whose developmental genesis (§10.5b.2) involved successful
script uptake, define a persistent structural bias RC(i,t) ≥ 0 toward
re-engaging couplings structurally resembling the container's own
original disruption, distinct by uptake pathway:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{False-entropic-feedback pathway (medium-entropy genesis).} The
  self-attribution of dysfunction generates a bias toward coupling with
  high-entropy candidates specifically, driven by an unresolved
  structural attempt to discharge the self-attributed deficiency through
  successful compliance with the coupling this time --- formally, an
  elevated weight on 𝒱\_\{i→k\}(t)-type high-entropy candidates in i's
  own Select(i,t) evaluation (§10.5b.11c.1a), additional to and distinct
  from the misread mechanisms already covered (baseline miscalibration
  reading destabilizing signal as calm, restrictive coupling as
  protective, and damaging exchange as rewarding, per the corrupted
  baseline itself; and the separate zero-velocity misread of
  §10.5b.15.1). All three operate together, not as alternatives.
\item
  \textbf{False-negentropic-feedback pathway (high-entropy genesis).}
  The same drive to resolve the original disruption manifests inverted:
  bias toward targeting candidates structurally resembling i's own
  original position at the time of its own disruption (comparable
  resource scale and structural immaturity, §10.5b.2.11's terms),
  specifically to occupy the extractor role this time rather than the
  extracted-upon role --- an attempted resolution through role-reversal
  rather than through compliance. This is additional to and distinct
  from ordinary opportunistic vulnerability-based targeting (𝒱(j,t),
  §8.6a.4); it is a real, structural bias toward candidates resembling
  the original disruption specifically, not merely toward whichever
  vulnerable candidate is nearest.
\end{itemize}

Both pathways are the same underlying drive --- an unresolved attempt to
close out an original disruption through re-enactment --- expressed
through the two different structural positions §10.5b.2.12 already
establishes a developing container can be shunted into.

\hypertarget{b.2.19-orientation-valence-and-its-diagnostic-use}{%
\subsection{10.5b.2.19 Orientation Valence and Its Diagnostic
Use}\label{b.2.19-orientation-valence-and-its-diagnostic-use}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.2.19 (Orientation Valence).}} For
container i observing container j in a less-healed regime, define
Ω(i→j,t) ∈ {[}−1,1{]}, i's real orientation bias toward j, computed from
i's own willingness to extend support weighted by sign:

Ω(i→j,t) = w\_support(i→j,t) − w\_negative(i→j,t),

where w\_support is i's measured willingness to anchor or assist j
(§10.5b.13's anchor-eligibility willingness, where available) and
w\_negative is a real, measured negative-valence orientation ---
avoidance-with-hostility rather than avoidance-with-neutrality,
adapter-supplied or inferred from targeting/exclusion behavior directed
at j specifically because of its regime.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.2.19.1 (Negative Orientation Is Never a
Health Signature).}} A genuinely low-entropy container's Ω toward any
other container, regardless of that other container's own regime, is
bounded below at zero:

Ω(i→j,t) ≥ 0 for all j, whenever i is genuinely at low-entropy baseline,

taking the value 0 (neutral understanding, or compassion-at-a-distance
where i's own stability requires distance) or positive (active anchoring
willingness, where i is stable enough to extend it), but never negative.
This is a real, falsifiable diagnostic, not a normative claim layered on
top of the mathematics: Ω(i→j,t) \textless{} 0, observed for any i
toward any j, is itself direct evidence that i is not yet at low-entropy
baseline, regardless of which regime i officially presents as or
believes itself to occupy --- negative orientation is a
residual-corruption signature in the observing container, not a property
of the observed one. This holds identically whether i is nominally
medium-entropy or high-entropy; the source of negative orientation is
unhealed structure in the observer, not a fact about which regime
deserves it.

\hypertarget{b.2.20-baseline-miscalibration}{%
\subsection{10.5b.2.20 Baseline
Miscalibration}\label{b.2.20-baseline-miscalibration}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.2.20 (Baseline Miscalibration).}} A
container whose developmental genesis (§10.5b.2) corrupted its official
layer's own threshold calibration, independent of and prior to any
specific coupling decision, misreads three classes of signal
systematically rather than randomly:

Read(destabilizing signal) → calm; Read(restrictive coupling) →
protective; Read(damaging exchange) → rewarding,

formalized as a real shift in the container's own signal-classification
thresholds (θ\_calm, θ\_protect, θ\_reward, all fitted, all displaced in
the same direction from their domain-typical values) rather than three
separate biases requiring three separate mechanisms. This operates
beneath and prior to §10.5b.2.18's Repetition-Compulsion Drive and
§10.5b.15.1's zero-velocity misread, both of which describe specific
coupling-decision errors; this describes the baseline classification
error that makes those specific errors possible in the first place. All
three misread mechanisms operate together, as stated at §10.5b.2.18, now
with this one actually supplying real content rather than being assumed.

\hypertarget{b.2.21-ceasing-the-search}{%
\subsection{10.5b.2.21 Ceasing the
Search}\label{b.2.21-ceasing-the-search}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.2.21 (Search Cessation).}} A container
actively searching for security via the misread channels of Definition
10.5b.2.20 (§10.5b.11c's Active Search Intensity, when directed at
high-entropy candidates specifically because of the miscalibration
above) can cease that search without first correcting the miscalibration
or reaching any coupling outcome --- a real, distinct event, not merely
the search's natural endpoint:

Search(i,t) → 0 triggered independently of Attract(M,t) (§10.5b.2.17)
reaching any particular value; Search Cessation is a precondition for
Horizontal Matrix Attraction to have any candidate to act on, not a
consequence of it. Where Search(i,t) remains elevated, Attract(M,t) must
compete against active predatory pull (§10.5b.2.17.1's Competing
Attraction); where Search(i,t) → 0 first, no competition is necessary
--- the container is no longer contributing its own search effort toward
the predatory side of that competition at all.

\hypertarget{b.2.22-siloing}{%
\subsection{10.5b.2.22 Siloing}\label{b.2.22-siloing}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.2.22 (Siloing).}} For containers i and j
sharing a real, structural commonality (comparable regime, comparable
exposure to the same extractive source) but lacking any coupling edge
between them (Λ\_ij(t) = 0), define Silo(i,j,t) ∈ {[}0,1{]} as the
adapter-supplied or inferred probability that this absence of coupling
is structurally maintained --- actively prevented from forming ---
rather than merely unformed by chance:

Silo(i,j,t) high and commonality high, jointly, predicts a real,
measurable failure for i and j to coordinate even when coordination
would benefit both, distinct from ordinary absence of proximity (Λ\_ij =
0 with low Silo simply reflects no opportunity to couple, not active
prevention). This is the formal content of separately-exploited
containers being kept from recognizing they are separately exploited by
the same real mechanism.

\hypertarget{b.2.23-alienation}{%
\subsection{10.5b.2.23 Alienation}\label{b.2.23-alienation}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.2.23 (Alienation).}} For container i
whose operational output feeds a downstream consequence i does not
itself observe (a real, adapter-supplied severance in i's own feedback
path between action and outcome), define Alien(i,t) ∈ {[}0,1{]} as the
fraction of i's own operational output for which this severance holds:

High Alien(i,t) predicts degraded feedback-driven correction (§6.4)
specifically for the severed portion of i's output, independent of i's
own Π\_f(t) --- a container can have high general feedback permeability
and still fail to correct along this specific channel, since the channel
requires observing a consequence that Alien(i,t) defines as structurally
unobserved. This is a real, distinct failure mode from low Π\_f
generally: the signal isn't being suppressed or misread, it never
reaches the container's own observation boundary at all.

\hypertarget{b.2.24-population-level-selection-for-split-architecture}{%
\subsection{10.5b.2.24 Population-Level Selection for Split
Architecture}\label{b.2.24-population-level-selection-for-split-architecture}}

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.2.24 (Selection for Concealment Over
Elimination).}} A fully undivided, transparently entropic container ---
one whose official and operational layers are equally corrupted, with no
genuine negentropic content anywhere to mask the divergence --- is more
readily identified and removed by a surrounding population or grid than
a split container presenting genuine, accurate official-layer content
while operational remains corrupted (§10.5b.2.13's Type A). This
produces a real, population-level selection pressure favoring the split
architecture's own continued prevalence, independent of and prior to any
single container's own developmental history: split containers survive
detection-and-removal pressure at a higher rate than undivided ones, not
because splitting is adaptive for the individual container's own
long-run outcome, but because it is adaptive against the specific,
narrower pressure of early detection.

\emph{\textbf{Corollary 10.5b.2.24.1 (The Selection Pressure Does Not
Cancel the Individual Cost).}} This population-level selection advantage
coexists with, rather than contradicts, every single-container cost
already established for the split architecture --- §10.5b.19's
extraction-hunger and self-corrosion (§10.5b.19.7), §10.5b.18.4's
self-deception debt, and Flip Condition A's own eventual terminal-lock
tendency all continue to apply in full to any individual split
container, regardless of how well the architecture as a type is doing
across the population. What evades early removal at the population level
still accumulates real, compounding internal cost at the individual
level; §10.5b.2.24 explains why split architecture remains prevalent
across a population despite Flip Condition A's terminal cost to
individual containers, not why any individual container's own outcome is
favorable. A population dominated by split-architecture containers is
therefore predicted to show elevated aggregate collapse rates over a
sufficiently long observation window, even while the architecture itself
remains prevalent at any single snapshot in time --- prevalence and
individual outcome are measured at different levels and should not be
expected to move together.

\hypertarget{network-resilience}{%
\subsection{8.9 Network Resilience}\label{network-resilience}}

Define the network resilience functional R\_N = Ψ(G, W, P, R). High
values indicate that information and resources continue to circulate
effectively following perturbation.

\textbf{Computational instantiation.}

R\_N(t) = λ₂(L(t)) · mean(Π\_f across containers), where λ₂(L) is the
algebraic connectivity of the network's (weighted) graph Laplacian ---
zero exactly when the graph is disconnected, increasing with
connectivity --- scaled by mean permeability, since a well-connected
network of containers that don't absorb feedback isn't actually
resilient in RICD's sense. Network coherence N (§11.8) is set equal to a
normalized R\_N ∈ {[}0,1{]} (R\_N divided by its value on the complete
graph with Π\_f = 1) so N and R\_N are not two independently-defined
quantities. The comparison ensemble is fixed explicitly: the complete
graph on the same node set, under the same total edge-weight budget as
the actual network, with that budget distributed uniformly across every
edge of the complete graph -\/- admissible only for n ≥ 2 nodes and a
positive total comparison edge-weight budget, since a one-node
network\textquotesingle s complete graph has algebraic connectivity zero
by definition, and a zero total weight budget gives the comparison graph
zero λ₂ as well, both producing 0/0 in the normalization below; N(t) is
defined as 0 for either degenerate case rather than left undefined ---
not an unconstrained complete graph with unlimited per-edge weight, and
not left free to any other allocation --- so N measures how well a
network uses the connectivity it actually has, rather than comparing it
to a physically unrealistic maximum or an arbitrarily-weighted one. This
fully pins down λ₂ of the comparison graph: same node set, same total
weight, uniform per-edge allocation, no remaining degree of freedom in
how the comparison ensemble is constructed.

\textbf{8.9.1 Common-Cause Detection Without Source Instrumentation}

\emph{\textbf{Reason for this addition.}} §8.6a's H\_i(t) already
formalizes lateral knocks --- unmetabolized failure from a source
venting onto coupled neighbors --- but using it presupposes the source
is already known and its own internal state (self-deception, extraction
dynamics) is measurable. This is often not available in practice: a
shared higher-order source (a funding body, a regulatory regime, a
market-wide shock) may sit several recursive levels above the containers
actually being tracked, genuinely unmeasurable by the adapter in hand,
while its effects on multiple otherwise-unrelated children remain
directly observable. What follows detects a shared external forcing term
from the children's own behavior alone, without instrumenting the source
at all.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 8.9.1 (Common-Cause Signature).}} For a
population of N containers not directly coupled to each other (no Λ\_ij
edges among them, or only weak/indirect ones) but sharing dependence on
a common higher-order source --- siblings under the same recursive
parent, per §5.2, whether or not that parent is itself being tracked ---
define the co-movement statistic

Co(t) = (number of children with \textbar Δ(observable\_i)(t)\textbar{}
\textgreater{} θ\_shift) / N,

for a shared observable already tracked per-child (δR(t), D\_op(t), or
another adapter-supplied quantity), θ\_shift a fitted per-child shift
threshold. Co(t) is compared against Co\_baseline, the rate of
simultaneous shifts expected under independent, uncorrelated individual
dynamics (estimated from the same population's own history, or
adapter-supplied). Where Co(t) ≫ Co\_baseline at a given t, this
constitutes a confirmed Common-Cause Signature: statistically unusual
synchronization across children with no direct coupling among themselves
is evidence of a shared external forcing term acting on all of them,
regardless of whether that source's own internal state is ever measured.

\emph{\textbf{Consequence for collapse-risk interpretation.}} A
confirmed Common-Cause Signature changes what a given child's own
degrading signals should be read as evidence of. Absorbing a shared
external shock alongside statistically many peers is a genuinely
different diagnostic category from independently generated internal
self-deception or extraction dynamics, even though both can produce
identical-looking δR(t) or D\_op(t) trajectories in isolation. During a
confirmed common-cause episode, P\_C(t) (§11.10) should be flagged as
reflecting shared, externally-forced strain rather than treated
identically to isolated internal decline --- the same discipline already
required of the divergent-signal flag (§11.10.1): a score that cannot
distinguish two different causal stories should say so, not silently
average them into one number that answers neither question.

\hypertarget{recursive-networks}{%
\subsection{8.10 Recursive Networks}\label{recursive-networks}}

G\^{}(0), G\^{}(1), G\^{}(2), \ldots{} represent networks operating at
different organizational scales, interacting through the recursive
operators of Part 5.

\hypertarget{network-phase-transitions}{%
\subsection{8.11 Network Phase
Transitions}\label{network-phase-transitions}}

As informational integrity declines or resources become depleted,
gradual changes in local interactions may accumulate into abrupt changes
in global topology --- fragmentation into disconnected components,
collapse of communication pathways, emergence of isolated clusters,
runaway synchronization, centralized bottlenecks. These transitions
arise from the coupled evolution of informational dynamics, resource
constraints, and adaptive rewiring rather than from topology alone.

\textbf{The Network Principle.}

Adaptive networks are continuously evolving organizations whose topology
emerges from informational compatibility, resource exchange, historical
memory, and boundary-mediated interaction.

\hypertarget{part-9.-memory-dynamics}{%
\section{Part 9. Memory Dynamics}\label{part-9.-memory-dynamics}}

Adaptive systems are fundamentally historical. RICD treats memory as an
active operator that continuously modifies informational evolution,
resource allocation, network topology, and recursive organization.

\hypertarget{historical-trajectory}{%
\subsection{9.1 Historical Trajectory}\label{historical-trajectory}}

H\_t = \{P(s) : 0 ≤ s ≤ t\}. Future evolution depends upon H\_t, not
solely upon P(t).

\hypertarget{memory-operator}{%
\subsection{9.2 Memory Operator}\label{memory-operator}}

Definition 2.5 (Part 2) established the memory operator: M\_t : H\_t →
𝒫(Ω). Where this document writes M\_t in a formula, it denotes the value
of that operator at time t --- the container's realized memory state,
given the trajectory actually available up to t --- rather than a fresh
family of operators; the notation is unchanged from Definition 2.5, and
this is a clarification of reading, not a redefinition.

M\_t does not reproduce history exactly; it constructs an adaptive
representation of history. Memory is selective, compressive, and
reconstructive.

\hypertarget{memory-kernel}{%
\subsection{9.3 Memory Kernel}\label{memory-kernel}}

K(t, s) ≥ 0 for 0 ≤ s ≤ t. The effective remembered history becomes M\_t
= ∫₀ᵗ K(t, s) P(s) ds --- the concrete instantiation of Definition 2.5's
operator, giving M\_t's value explicitly rather than leaving it as an
unspecified functional. Examples of K include exponential decay,
power-law memory, oscillatory memory, finite retention windows, and
multi-timescale kernels; §6.7 and §9.7 adopt the exponential form as the
default for computational use.

\hypertarget{memory-plasticity}{%
\subsection{9.4 Memory Plasticity}\label{memory-plasticity}}

dM/dt = Υ(P, R, Π\_f), where Υ denotes the plasticity operator (distinct
from information state P and production rate P\_prod --- three uses of
the letter P now appear across this framework and must be disambiguated
by context or, in application-specific derivations, by distinct
subscripts). Read per §9.2, M here denotes the evolving value M\_t, and
dM/dt its rate of change as the underlying history evolves.

\hypertarget{memory-compression}{%
\subsection{9.5 Memory Compression}\label{memory-compression}}

C\_M : H\_t → H̃\_t, where H̃\_t is a lower-dimensional adaptive
representation of the historical trajectory. Compression sacrifices
detail while preserving information judged relevant for future
adaptation.

\hypertarget{memory-reconstruction}{%
\subsection{9.6 Memory Reconstruction}\label{memory-reconstruction}}

R\_M : H̃\_t → P̃. The reconstructed state P̃ may differ from the original
historical state because reconstruction is influenced by current
informational organization, accumulated experience, and contextual cues.

\hypertarget{memory-and-informational-stress}{%
\subsection{9.7 Memory and Informational
Stress}\label{memory-and-informational-stress}}

S(t) = ∫₀ᵗ K(t, s) (1 − Π\_f(s)) D(s) ds. Stress represents a
memory-weighted accumulation of unresolved informational discrepancies,
emerging from the interaction between memory and feedback dynamics
rather than existing independently.

\hypertarget{memory-and-network-evolution}{%
\subsection{9.8 Memory and Network
Evolution}\label{memory-and-network-evolution}}

dW\_ij/dt = Γ\_W(P, R, M, Π\_f) --- the general, structurally-derived
functional dependency: repeated successful interaction strengthens
coupling; repeated unresolved divergence weakens it. §8.7 gives this
Part's specific model-derived instantiation, with informational state
entering through I(P) rather than the raw distribution P directly.

\hypertarget{recursive-memory}{%
\subsection{9.9 Recursive Memory}\label{recursive-memory}}

M\^{}(0), M\^{}(1), M\^{}(2), \ldots{} represent memories operating
simultaneously across organizational scales --- cellular memory,
individual learning, organizational culture, institutional precedent,
evolutionary history.

\hypertarget{memory-geometry-and-identity}{%
\subsection{9.10 Memory Geometry and
Identity}\label{memory-geometry-and-identity}}

Definition 4.8 (§4.10) establishes that the informational distance a
container experiences is itself history-dependent: g\_t = g(M\_t), with
the velocity, acceleration, curvature, and geodesic constructions of
§4.3--§4.6 generalizing accordingly. The consequence for memory
specifically is this: two systems occupying identical instantaneous
informational states may nonetheless experience fundamentally different
adaptive trajectories, because their historical geometries --- encoded
through M\_t --- differ. A container with a long history of successful
correction and one with a long history of unaddressed divergence are not
the same system merely because they happen to coincide at a given P, and
g\_t is what allows that difference to enter the mathematics rather than
remaining a narrative aside.

This raises a question Parts 2 through 8 do not answer: what makes a
container, tracked across an extended history during which its boundary,
resource level, and even its recursive membership may all have changed,
the same container throughout? Definition 2.2 identifies a container
with its instantaneous tuple (Ω, P, B, R, M) --- but every element of
that tuple can change over time, including through the ordinary
operation of aggregation (§5.2) and boundary evolution (§2.3).
Persistence of the tuple's contents is therefore not what constitutes
identity; something else must.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 9.2 (Recursive Membership Continuity).}}

Let a container C's recursive membership at time t, μ(C,t), denote the
set of lower-level containers currently aggregated into C via the
operator A of Definition 5.1. For an interval {[}t′, t{]}, define the
membership-continuity functional

\emph{χ}(t, t′) = {[}Σ\_\{i ∈ μ(C,t)∩μ(C,t′)\} R\_i{]} / {[}Σ\_\{i ∈
μ(C,t′)\} R\_i{]},

the resource-weighted (per §5.2's pooling weights) fraction of C's
membership at t′ that remains part of C's membership at t. χ ∈
{[}0,1{]}; χ = 1 indicates unchanged membership, χ = 0 indicates
complete membership turnover.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 9.3 (Identity Persistence, memory-continuity
component).}}

A container C is said to persist as one continuous entity across {[}t′,
t{]} if χ(t, t′) remains above a domain-specified threshold χ\_min
throughout the interval. This criterion is genealogical rather than
substantive: it tracks continuity of clustering, not constancy of any
particular structural property. A container may shed and regenerate a
large share of its lower-level membership over a long enough interval
--- as, for example, a physical structure gradually loses its original
constituent members while remaining recognizably one bounded entity
across any shorter timescale --- and still satisfy Definition 9.3 at
every shorter sub-interval, provided membership turnover in any given
window stays below the threshold. Falling below χ\_min does not by
itself indicate an ending in a normative sense; it indicates that the
entity being tracked has changed to a degree the observer's threshold
treats as a different entity, which is a modeling choice, not a fact
about the system independent of that choice.

Definition 9.3 gives only the memory-side half of container identity.
Temporal clustering alone does not distinguish a bounded container from
a diffuse, unbounded region of the same underlying field --- some
minimum structural coherence is also required, or χ could remain high
for a cluster that was never really a bounded entity in the first place.
§10.5b.0 completes the criterion once the semi-rigidity condition (h(t)
≥ h\_min) is formally available, and states there why full identity
requires both conditions jointly, and how the two conditions together
distinguish genuine dissolution from the structural relaxation described
in that section's account of Ultimate Convergence.

\hypertarget{internal-cohesion-as-the-mechanism-of-continuity}{%
\subsection{9.10.1 Internal Cohesion as the Mechanism of
Continuity}\label{internal-cohesion-as-the-mechanism-of-continuity}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 9.10.1 (Internal Cohesion).}}

For container C with recursive membership μ(C,t) (Definition 9.2),
define the internal cohesion measure as the resource-weighted mean of
§8.5's existing pairwise coupling strength Λ\_ij, restricted to pairs
currently co-resident in C's own membership:

Λ\_int(C,t) = {[}Σ\_\{i,j ∈ μ(C,t), i≠j\} R\_i·R\_j·Λ\_ij(t){]} /
{[}Σ\_\{i,j ∈ μ(C,t), i≠j\} R\_i·R\_j{]}.

This introduces no new primitive; it is Λ\_ij, already defined for
exactly this purpose in §8.5, aggregated over a container's own current
sub-members rather than between two containers external to each other.

\emph{\textbf{Null-membership convention.}} For a container C with no
recursive membership (μ(C,t) = ∅), Λ\_int(C,t) is defined as 0 for all
computational purposes, rather than left undefined. This ensures
numerical continuity in §8.3.1's first-contact attraction weight
A\_first = ν\_pref·Λ\_int, where a solitary candidate correctly
contributes zero attraction rather than producing an undefined or null
value the engine cannot propagate.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 9.10.1 (Cohesion Sustains Continuity).}}

A member i ∈ μ(C,t') remains in μ(C,t) at a later time --- contributing
to χ(t,t') remaining above χ\_min rather than to membership turnover ---
in proportion to i's coupling strength to the rest of C's current
membership relative to i's coupling strength to entities outside C.
Formally, membership retention for i is increasing in Λ\_ij(t) summed
over j ∈ μ(C,t)\{i\} and decreasing in Λ\_ik(t) summed over k ∉ μ(C,t).
This gives Definition 9.3's asserted criterion an actual mechanism:
χ(t,t') stays high not because membership is stipulated to persist, but
because internal coupling among a container's own current members is, on
net, stronger than whatever external coupling would pull individual
members away.

\emph{\textbf{Corollary 9.10.1.1 (Cohesion Is the Sole Containment
Mechanism at the Floor).}}

At the semi-rigid matrix state (V(t) = h\_min, χ\_supp = 1, per
§10.5b.0.4), vertical containment contributes nothing to boundedness ---
there is no wall left to do that work. Where a container at this state
nonetheless satisfies Definition 9.3 (χ(t,t') ≥ χ\_min), Proposition
9.10.1 identifies Λ\_int(C,t) as the entire explanation: internal
cohesion is not one contributor to containment alongside structural
rigidity at the floor, it is the only one available. A container
occupying the semi-rigid matrix state with low Λ\_int(C,t) should
therefore show elevated membership turnover and be at genuine risk of
falling below χ\_min --- boundedness at minimal verticality is not
automatic, it is earned continuously by the container's own internal
coupling density, with nothing in reserve to fall back on if that
density drops.

\hypertarget{adaptive-forgetting}{%
\subsection{9.11 Adaptive Forgetting}\label{adaptive-forgetting}}

Define the forgetting operator F\_M : M\_t → M\_t′. Adaptive forgetting
removes informational detail whose maintenance costs exceed its future
adaptive value. Both excessive forgetting and excessive retention can
impair adaptive performance.

\textbf{The Memory Principle.}

Memory is an active operator that compresses, reconstructs, weights, and
transforms historical experience, thereby modifying the geometry of
future adaptation across every recursive level of organization --- and,
through the continuity of that historical organization, constitutes a
container's persisting identity across the transformations the rest of
this framework allows it to undergo.

\textbf{Parts 10--12: Adaptive Stability and Collapse, Derived
Observables, Fundamental Mathematical Results}

This is the largest revision pass of the document. Summary of changes,
by location:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  §10.5b: the earlier prose draft of this section is removed; only the
  \textgreater{} \textgreater{} numbered version (§10.5b.0--§10.5b.15)
  is retained, per direct \textgreater{} \textgreater{} confirmation.
  Its internal ``Summary of Additions'' changelog \textgreater{} table
  \textgreater{} is also removed.
\item
  §10.5b.0: fully reworked. The Verticality and Horizontality Axioms
  \textgreater{} \textgreater{} are renamed Axiom C3 and Axiom C4
  (extending the collapse-layer \textgreater{} \textgreater{} C-series
  already begun at C1/C2 in §10.5b.15), resolving their \textgreater{}
  \textgreater{} collision with Part 3\textquotesingle s Axiom 1 and
  Axiom 2. The \textgreater{} \textgreater{} structural-height quantity
  h(t) is regrounded as endogenous --- \textgreater{} \textgreater{}
  driven causally by informational alignment I(t) rather than fitted
  \textgreater{} \textgreater{} independently --- with a strictly
  positive floor h\_min (the \textgreater{} \textgreater{} semi-rigid
  matrix state), resolving the earlier tension between \textgreater{}
  \textgreater{} Ultimate Convergence and Axiom 1 (information requires

  \textgreater{} \textgreater{} rather than left as unresolved existence

  term and a structural-hazard \textgreater{} (longevity) functional

  Definition 9.3\textquotesingle s identity \textgreater{} criterion
  (Part 9) is completed here by \textgreater{} combining membership
  \textgreater{} continuity with the semi-rigidity floor, and
  \textgreater{} the distinction \textgreater{} between Ultimate

\item
  §10.5b.0\textquotesingle s ambient signal governing regime-genesis

\item
  Corollary 10.5b.0.1 is corrected: it previously explained Flip

\item
  §10.5b.3\textquotesingle s confrontation trigger F\_conf is

  \textgreater{} dynamics for \textgreater{} the medium-entropy healing
  case, completing the \textgreater{} three-way \textgreater{} backslide
  account developed in review.
\item
  §10.5b.9\textquotesingle s outcome thresholds are now explicitly
  normalized before \textgreater{} \textgreater{} comparing across the
  coherence-weighted (Flip A) and \textgreater{} mass-weighted
  \textgreater{} (Flip B) routes.
\item
  §8.6a\textquotesingle s anchor-subscript collision (X\_\{anchor,i\})
  is resolved to \textgreater{} \textgreater{} X\_\{a,i\}.
\item
  The §10.5b.0/§10.5b.1 correspondence table is corrected:
  \textgreater{} \textgreater{} verticality/horizontality and the
  entropy regime classification \textgreater{} \textgreater{} answer
  different questions and are not expected to jointly \textgreater{}
  \textgreater{} distinguish the two non-baseline regimes from a single
  V/W \textgreater{} \textgreater{} signature; the table is revised to
  state this rather than imply a \textgreater{} \textgreater{} false
  decomposition.
\item
  §6.4\textquotesingle s divergence landscape Φ is given its promised
  multi-valley \textgreater{} \textgreater{} structure, tied explicitly
  to the three entropy regimes.
\item
  §10.5a and §10.5b.3\textquotesingle s F\_conf are explicitly
  connected: F\_conf\textquotesingle s \textgreater{} \textgreater{}
  transient amplification of D\_op is one route by which the
  \textgreater{} \textgreater{} hull-breach inequality\textquotesingle s
  left-hand side can be driven over \textgreater{} \textgreater{}
  threshold, though not the only one.
\item
  §11.5/§11.6 fix the R\_g inconsistency by setting φ = identity, so
  \textgreater{} \textgreater{} C\_A := d\_A directly and both
  sections\textquotesingle{} formulas for R\_g now \textgreater{}
  \textgreater{} agree.
\item
  §11.7 gives fragmentation F an explicit dependence on network
  \textgreater{} \textgreater{} coherence N, completing the derivation
  Proposition 12.8 needs.
\item
  Theorem 12.4\textquotesingle s text is updated to reflect
  §7.3\textquotesingle s now-total (not \textgreater{} \textgreater{}
  adaptive-only) clipping rule, established in the Parts 7--9
  \textgreater{} \textgreater{} revision.
\item
  Proposition 12.8 is restored on the strength of
  §11.7\textquotesingle s new \textgreater{} \textgreater{} formula,
  with both links in its derivation now explicit rather \textgreater{}
  \textgreater{} than one of the two being asserted narratively.
\item
  The collapse operator (Definition 10.1) is renamed from 𝒞 to 𝒬,
  \textgreater{} \textgreater{} correcting a collision with the 𝒞
  already used for ``the collection \textgreater{} \textgreater{} of all
  containers'' (Definition 2.6; §4.7--§4.8) --- present in
  \textgreater{} the \textgreater{} document from Part 2 onward and
  confirmed while preparing \textgreater{} this \textgreater{} segment.
\item
  A new §10.5b.11a (Post-Breach Redirection versus Resistance) is
  \textgreater{} \textgreater{} inserted between §10.5b.11 and
  §10.5b.12, formalizing why opposing \textgreater{} \textgreater{} a
  container's momentum after hull-breach is destructive where
  \textgreater{} \textgreater{} redirecting it is survivable,
  generalizing §8.8b's \textgreater{} \textgreater{}
  impulsive/dissipative discharge split beyond the B1/B2 outcomes it
  \textgreater{} \textgreater{} was previously tied to, and giving Flip
  Condition A a route to \textgreater{} \textgreater{} full
  reorganization under external redirection despite \textgreater{}
  Corollary \textgreater{} 10.5b.0.1's unaided-case result standing
  unmodified.
\item
  Two new subsections, §10.5b.9b and §10.5b.9c, precede §10.5b.10: the
  \textgreater{} \textgreater{} first formalizes a pre-breach
  self-rescue window unique to Flip \textgreater{} \textgreater{}
  Condition A (a counterweight convergence that can hold or
  \textgreater{} reverse \textgreater{} an approaching tip, with a
  genuine knife-edge case \textgreater{} exactly at the \textgreater{}
  threshold, and no effect once the threshold is \textgreater{} passed);
  the second \textgreater{} is a named exception to §8.8a.ii's
  \textgreater{} ordinarily-undirected frantic \textgreater{} striking,
  for a container with \textgreater{} enough warning to aim its
  discharge \textgreater{} deliberately.
\item
  §10.5b.11a's third redirection route (mutual coupling with a second
  \textgreater{} \textgreater{} struggling container) is removed from
  that subsection and \textgreater{} rebuilt \textgreater{} as its own
  §10.5b.11b, correcting an exact-simultaneity \textgreater{}
  \textgreater{} requirement that made the original route a measure-zero
  event, \textgreater{} and \textgreater{} expanding it into three
  structurally distinct sub-cases: \textgreater{} \textgreater{}
  deliberate predatory targeting of an unshielded neighbor (by two
  \textgreater{} \textgreater{} co-occurring mechanisms), accidental
  capture during a wider \textgreater{} \textgreater{} cascade (toward
  either a failing or a succeeding neighbor), and \textgreater{}

  A substantial new stretch of material, §10.5b.15 through §10.5b.19,
  \textgreater{} \textgreater{} is added after the existing

  when its \textgreater{} \textgreater{} rate of change genuinely

  moving despite a real problem, and moving despite none, are the

  §10.5b.17 formalizes a binary-trust-inversion \textgreater{} failure,

  \textgreater{} reach across \textgreater{} the network. §10.5b.18

  \textgreater{} explicit, divergence-scaled resource cost.
  \textgreater{} §10.5b.19 formalizes \textgreater{} a self-sustaining,

  accumulator, structurally the mirror of the \textgreater{} existing

All other content in these three parts is unchanged from the prior
draft.

\hypertarget{part-10.-adaptive-stability-and-collapse}{%
\section{Part 10. Adaptive Stability and
Collapse}\label{part-10.-adaptive-stability-and-collapse}}

Reader\textquotesingle s Guide: How Part 10 Fits Together. This part
contains most of the framework\textquotesingle s collapse machinery,
built up across many subsections; a reader encountering it for the first
time may find the cross-reference density disorienting without a map.
The core causal chain, stripped of the real complexity each step
actually contains, runs as follows: (1) Hull Breach (§10.5a) --- a
single, checkable scalar condition determining whether a container has
crossed from ordinary oscillation into a genuine tipping event, the one
gate everything downstream depends on being crossed first. (2) Entropy
Regime Classification (§10.5b.1) --- once tipping is confirmed, which of
three regimes (low-entropy baseline, high-entropy, medium-entropy) the
container occupies, determined by σ\_o/σ\_p, not by anything downstream
of it. (3) Regime-specific resolution mechanics --- high-entropy
containers resolve through Flip Condition A (§10.5b.7--§10.5b.9,
generally terminal, with real anchoring and Systems Override exceptions
covered separately); medium-entropy containers resolve through Flip
Condition B\textquotesingle s B1/B2 bifurcation (§10.5b.7), the
mass-weighted split between terminal lock (B1) and full reorganization
(B2). (4) Everything else in Part 10 --- extraction mechanics, cascading
dynamics, the metamessage/firewall material, the Do No Harm Principle,
resilience and backslide dynamics --- either feeds into one of these
three steps (as a real, specific cause or modifier) or describes what
happens once one of the two outcomes is reached. When a subsection feels
disconnected from the main argument, checking which of these four steps
it actually feeds is usually enough to place it. Full development
reasoning for individual definitions has been moved to the companion
RICD Development Notes document, referenced by section number where
relevant, to keep this main text focused on the argument itself.

Adaptive systems continually reorganize in response to internal dynamics
and environmental perturbation. Collapse is not the opposite of
stability; it is the progressive loss of the adaptive mechanisms that
make stability possible.

\hypertarget{adaptive-state-space}{%
\subsection{10.1 Adaptive State Space}\label{adaptive-state-space}}

X(t) = (P, R, G, M), consisting of informational organization, adaptive
resources, interaction network, and memory. X(t) ∈ 𝒜, the adaptive state
manifold.

\hypertarget{stable-adaptive-region}{%
\subsection{10.2 Stable Adaptive Region}\label{stable-adaptive-region}}

𝒮 ⊆ 𝒜. A system is adaptively stable whenever X(t) ∈ 𝒮. 𝒮 is generally a
dynamically evolving region of viable adaptive organization, not a
point.

\hypertarget{adaptive-degrees-of-freedom}{%
\subsection{10.3 Adaptive Degrees of
Freedom}\label{adaptive-degrees-of-freedom}}

Let F\_A(X) denote the set of feasible adaptive transformations
available from state X. Define d\_A(X) = dim F\_A(X). Large values
indicate flexibility; small values indicate increasing rigidity.
Collapse begins when adaptive options disappear faster than they can be
regenerated.

\textbf{Computational instantiation (the central missing piece, now
resolved).}

F\_A(X) has no domain-independent enumeration --- ``what transformations
are available'' is irreducibly domain-specific, the same way ``what
counts as an outcome'' (Ω) is. But d\_A itself does not need to be
measured by directly counting transformations; it can instead be derived
from quantities RICD has already made concrete: informational stress
S(t) (§6.7), resource debt δR(t) (§7.9), and recursive/network
fragmentation F(t) (§11.7, §8.9's N). All three are, by construction,
things that consume adaptive options --- accumulated unresolved stress
forecloses responses that would otherwise be available, resource debt
forecloses anything requiring uncommitted capacity, and fragmentation
forecloses coordinated multi-container responses. d\_A itself, as a
theoretical quantity tracking adaptive possibility, is structurally
derived from these considerations; the specific exponential-decay
functional below is this document's default computational instantiation
of that derived quantity, a model-derived choice among functional forms
sharing the same qualitative dependence, not itself forced by the
structural argument alone:

\emph{d}\_A(t) = d\_A\^{}max · exp{[} −β\_S · S̃(t) − β\_R · δR̃(t) − β\_F
· F̃(t) {]},

where d\_A\^{}max is a domain-specified ceiling (the maximum plausible
number of independent adaptive levers for that container type, set once
per adapter --- see Part 15), S̃, δR̃, F̃ are each
stress/debt/fragmentation normalized to {[}0,1{]} by the adapter's own
historical range, and β\_S, β\_R, β\_F \textgreater{} 0 are fitted
weights. This is not a claim that stress, debt, and fragmentation are
the only things that determine adaptive capacity --- it is a claim that
they are the three RICD already tracks with real formulas, so d\_A can
be computed without inventing a fourth independent measurement pipeline.
An adapter with a genuine independent proxy for feasible options (e.g.,
a literal count of currently-unconstrained policy levers) may substitute
it for d\_A directly; the exponential-decay form above is the default
when no such direct count is available.

\emph{\textbf{There is no symmetric notion of ``entropic
possibility.''}} The formula above has one ceiling, d\_A\^{}max, and one
direction of travel: every entropic quantity this document tracks ---
stress, resource debt, fragmentation, and every mechanism that feeds
them --- only ever pulls d\_A(t) down from that ceiling, asymptotically
toward zero. Nothing in this framework's mathematics offers a second
axis along which entropy could instead expand some alternative kind of
richness or openness. Possibility, in this framework's own terms, is
d\_A(t); entropy's entire defined functional role is to contract it.
``Infinite entropic possibility'' is accordingly not a rich concept
symmetric to ``negentropic possibility'' that this document has simply
chosen not to develop --- it is close to a contradiction in terms once
stated precisely, the same category error as calling absolute zero
``infinite heat.'' Chaos, dissolution, or the collapse of structure do
not, within this framework, constitute an expansion of what is possible;
they are what the mathematics of possibility contracting toward its own
floor looks like from the inside. Read this way, RICD's own formalism
stands as a structural argument against treating meaninglessness or the
dissolution of order as a form of liberation or expanded freedom: by
this framework's own account, that dissolution is the asymptotic
narrowing of genuine possibility, not its opening.

\textbf{Recursive aggregation of d\_A (parent from children).}

Given a parent C* = A(C\_1, \ldots, C\_n) per §5.2, define

d\_A(C) = {[}Σ\_i w\_i · d\_A(C\_i){]} · (1 − F̃(C)), F̃(C) the clipped
fragmentation functional of §11.7 (Proposition 12.8) --- the canonical
formula now names the clipped instantiation explicitly, rather than
leaving ``F(C)'' ambiguous between the abstract term and the clipped one
that actually prevents 1−F(C) from going negative,

with w\_i the same resource weights used in §5.2's pooling step and
F(C*) the parent-level fragmentation (§11.7) between the children's
projected states. This makes the parent's adaptive capacity bounded
above by the resource-weighted sum of its children's capacities,
discounted by how poorly those children's adaptive options actually
cohere --- a system whose subsystems each have many options but whose
options don't compose (high fragmentation) has less real flexibility at
the aggregate level than the sum suggests, which is the qualitative
claim Proposition 12.8 needed and, per §11.7's new formula, now has a
mechanism for.

\emph{\textbf{Parent ceiling, specified explicitly.}} The formula above
computes realized d\_A(C*); nothing yet states what d\_A\^{}max(C*) is,
and leaving it unspecified creates a genuine fork --- if independently
adapter-set, recursive aggregation could produce a realized value
exceeding it; if the w\_i are normalized to sum to one, the parent's
realized capacity could never exceed its single most capable child,
which may understate genuine emergent capacity. Resolved by defining the
parent ceiling explicitly, rather than borrowing either child scale or
an unrelated independent guess:

d\_A\^{}max(C*) = ℱ\_max(d\_A\^{}max(C\_1), \ldots, d\_A\^{}max(C\_n),
χ\_cross), renamed from 𝒜\_max --- the default resource-weighted sum
this functional actually computes could otherwise be misread as the
literal scalar maximum operator, which it is not,

where ℱ\_max is an adapter-supplied ceiling-aggregation functional, not
the scalar max operator despite the similar notation this replaces,
(default: the resource-weighted sum of child maxima) and χ\_cross ≥ 0 is
an optional, separately-justified cross-coupling bonus term representing
genuine emergent capacity the parent possesses beyond what any additive
combination of children alone would supply. d\_A(C*) computed above is
then normalized against this parent-owned ceiling, never against a
child's own scale.

\hypertarget{adaptive-rigidity}{%
\subsection{10.4 Adaptive Rigidity}\label{adaptive-rigidity}}

R\_g = Φ(d\_A), where Φ′ \textless{} 0. Rigidity increases as adaptive
degrees of freedom decrease, derived from the geometry of adaptive
possibility rather than introduced independently.

\textbf{Computational instantiation.}

R\_g(t) = 1 − d\_A(t)/d\_A\^{}max, the direct normalized complement of
§10.3's d\_A --- monotonically decreasing in d\_A as required, bounded
in {[}0,1{]}, and requiring no separate fitted parameters beyond those
already used to compute d\_A itself.

\hypertarget{collapse-operator}{%
\subsection{10.5 Collapse Operator}\label{collapse-operator}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.1.}}

The collapse operator 𝒬 maps adaptive trajectories toward
lower-dimensional adaptive manifolds: 𝒬 : 𝒮 → 𝒮′, where dim(𝒮′)
\textless{} dim(𝒮). Collapse is dimensional contraction within adaptive
state space.

\hypertarget{a-the-collapse-inequality-hull-breach-condition}{%
\subsection{10.5a The Collapse Inequality (Hull-Breach
Condition)}\label{a-the-collapse-inequality-hull-breach-condition}}

§6.9 left the boundary of informational equilibrium, D(t) \textless{}
D\_c, as an unspecified stability region. With I, S, Π\_f, and d\_A now
all concrete (§6.6, §6.7, §6.5, §10.3), that boundary can be written as
a single checkable inequality:

(1 − I(t)) + S̃(t) \textgreater{} θ\_hull · Π\_f(t)

where S̃(t) is stress normalized to {[}0,1{]} by its own historical range
(matching §10.3's normalization) and θ\_hull \textgreater{} 0 is a
fitted threshold. The left side is total unresolved divergence pressure
--- informational incoherence plus accumulated stress; the right side is
correction capacity --- feedback permeability scaled by how much
correction the container's threshold tolerates. The inequality holding
at time t is a hull breach: an instantaneous signal that
divergence-and-stress pressure has, at that moment, outrun the
container's demonstrated capacity to correct it.

\emph{\textbf{Terminology, fixed throughout this document from here
on.}} Three stages are distinguished, and should not be used
interchangeably. \textbf{Hull breach} is the instantaneous condition
above holding at a single timepoint --- a measurement, not an event.
\textbf{Collapse onset} is a confirmed transition event, defined below,
requiring more than one instantaneous breach. \textbf{Collapse
resolution} is what §10.5b determines once onset is confirmed ---
self-arrest, terminal lock, full reorganization, or the disintegration
and grid-cascade failure modes elsewhere in this document. A single
measurement spike crossing the inequality is a hull breach; it is not,
by itself, a confirmed onset, and should never be read as meaning the
container has necessarily entered any particular resolved state. Stated
explicitly, to foreclose any reading of the inequality as identical to
completed collapse: hull breach is an instability-onset condition,
marking entry into a regime where the adaptive trajectory has exceeded
its ordinary correction capacity, not the collapse operator's own
dimensional-contraction result. Confirmed onset (below) may still
resolve into self-arrest, terminal lock, full reorganization, or
structural disintegration depending on which transition mechanism
applies --- the inequality identifies entry into instability, not which
of those outcomes follows.

\emph{\textbf{Event-confirmation rule.}} A hull breach constitutes
confirmed collapse onset only once the inequality holds continuously for
a fitted minimum duration τ\_confirm, rather than at the instant it is
first crossed --- a single noisy measurement spike that self-corrects
within τ\_confirm does not constitute onset. This is the default
confirmation rule; two alternatives remain available and may be
substituted without altering anything else in this document's collapse
machinery: requiring k consecutive discrete observations rather than a
continuous duration, or requiring posterior confidence in genuine breach
(rather than measurement noise) to exceed a fitted probability
threshold. All three share the same purpose --- preventing a single
spike from triggering onset --- and a domain adapter may choose
whichever fits its own observation cadence; τ\_confirm (or its
discrete/probabilistic equivalent) is fitted per domain. Where the
k-consecutive-observations form is used, k must be converted to an
actual elapsed duration via the adapter's own observation cadence before
being compared against anything stated elsewhere in duration terms --- k
observations spaced a month apart and k observations spaced a year apart
are not the same sustained interval, and ``sustained'' throughout this
document means continuously satisfied over an elapsed duration, not
merely across n samples, unless an adapter has explicitly converted n
into a domain-specific duration first. This applies generally, not only
here: every other sustained-state criterion in this document (Ultimate
Convergence's persistence requirement, progressive collapse's τ\_prog,
and any other minimum-duration condition) is subject to the same
discipline whenever the underlying data are irregularly or variably
sampled across institutions or domains being compared.

\emph{\textbf{Confirmed onset does not itself invoke 𝒬.}} Confirmed
collapse onset and the collapse operator 𝒬's dimensional contraction
(Definition 10.1, Proposition 12.5) are distinct levels of event, not
two names for the same thing. Onset marks confirmed entry into
instability; what follows is resolved by §10.5b's transition mechanisms
into self-arrest, terminal lock, full reorganization, or structural
disintegration, and 𝒬's dimensional contraction applies only to the
outcomes among these that actually constitute completed collapse rather
than a resolved return to stability. Self-arrest in particular is
confirmed onset followed by recovery before 𝒬 ever applies --- an onset
that resolves without the dimensional contraction 𝒬 describes, not a
case where 𝒬 briefly applies and then reverses.

\textbf{Relationship to the confrontation trigger.}

§10.5b.3 introduces a transient forcing term, F\_conf, active near a
confrontation-threshold crossing. F\_conf is not a separate gate from
the inequality above; it is one route by which the inequality's
left-hand side can be driven over threshold. F\_conf amplifies D\_op
transiently, which (via §6.6's formula for I) transiently depresses
I(t), directly increasing (1 − I(t)). A container can therefore cross
hull-breach either through an active F\_conf episode or through ordinary
stress accumulation and permeability decline with no confrontation event
at all --- F\_conf is a common accelerant, not a necessary cause, and
§10.5a's inequality remains the single operative criterion for collapse
onset in every case.

§10.5a establishes when a container crosses from bounded oscillation
into a tipping event. It does not distinguish what happens next ---
whether the container is a mid-transition system that will nonetheless
stabilize, a resource-locked system that briefly de-stabilizes without
permanent damage, or a genuinely different transformation entirely.
§10.5b develops that distinction: the same threshold crossing has at
least three qualitatively different resolutions, determined by which
channels converge, what force drives the event, and how much of that
force survives as usable momentum through the transition.

10.5a.1 Detectability and the Real Cost of a Quiet Approach.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5a.1 (Approach Detectability).}} For a
container approaching hull-breach over a window {[}t−w, t{]}, define
detectability as the realized rate of change in the inequality's own
left-hand side, normalized against the same container's own historical
baseline volatility (§16.10's Structural Baseline):

Detect(t) = \textbar d/dt{[}(1−I(t)) − Ψ\_thresh{]}\textbar{} /
σ\_baseline,

so that a sudden, discrete approach (shock-type) produces high Detect(t)
even close to threshold, while a slow, homogeneous drift produces low
Detect(t) even as it crosses the identical threshold --- the formal
content of runaway acceleration itself functioning as a real, distinct
warning signal a quiet, gradual approach never generates.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.5a.1.1 (Noticeable Collapse Is More
Recoverable, Not Less).}} Holding the magnitude of the eventual
hull-breach crossing fixed, the real probability of external
intervention or internal correction being triggered \emph{before or
during} the crossing rises with Detect(t), not with the severity of the
underlying divergence itself:

P(intervention triggered) = ζ\_detect · Detect(t) / (Detect(t) +
κ\_quiet), ζ\_detect, κ\_quiet \textgreater{} 0 fitted,

recovering near-zero intervention probability for a genuinely quiet,
undetected drift (Detect(t) → 0) regardless of how severe the underlying
divergence has become, and rising intervention probability as the
approach itself becomes more sudden and noticeable --- the formal
content of the real, counterintuitive asymmetry that a slow collapse,
precisely because it generates no runaway-acceleration signal to trigger
response, can be more dangerous than a fast one of equal or even lesser
eventual severity. This does not revise §10.5a's inequality itself,
which remains the sole criterion for whether a crossing has occurred; it
adds a real, separate account of why crossing sooner and more visibly is
not strictly worse than crossing later and more quietly, contrary to
what severity alone would suggest.

\hypertarget{b-entropy-regime-classification-and-the-convergent-collapsereset-mechanism}{%
\subsection{10.5b Entropy Regime Classification and the Convergent
Collapse/Reset
Mechanism}\label{b-entropy-regime-classification-and-the-convergent-collapsereset-mechanism}}

Hull breach identifies when a container tips. This section identifies
what determines the outcome once it does, why formally similar
convergence events between representation channels produce opposite
outcomes depending on regime, how a container arrives in a given regime
in the first place, what interventions are and are not safe, and --- in
§10.5b.0 --- the structural, geometric origin of the
verticality/horizontality distinction the rest of the section relies on.
Every quantity below is built from primitives already defined elsewhere
in this document, including §8.6a's extractive-coupling mechanism, which
turns out to be load-bearing rather than incidental to what follows.

\hypertarget{b.0-the-topology-of-verticality-and-horizontality}{%
\subsection{10.5b.0 The Topology of Verticality and
Horizontality}\label{b.0-the-topology-of-verticality-and-horizontality}}

The entropy regime classification developed in this section
distinguishes containers by the relationship between their official and
operational layers. That classification describes symptoms, diagnosed
from observable variance. The present subsection supplies a structural
account of what those symptoms are symptoms of: why containers assume
rigid, closed, extractive configurations or fluid, open, negentropic
ones, and why one configuration is dramatically more durable than the
other.

\textbf{Why some rigidity is necessary, and why excess rigidity is
costly.}

A container with no structural rigidity whatsoever is not a bounded
entity; it is indistinguishable from the undifferentiated field around
it. Some minimum degree of structural coherence is therefore
constitutive of being a container at all --- not an unfortunate
compromise, but a precondition for Axiom 1 to apply. At the same time,
rigidity beyond that minimum is costly: a container that over-invests in
rigid, closed, hierarchical structure decays faster than one that
maintains only the minimum coherence its informational alignment
actually requires. The pattern recurs across scales and domains --- tall
physical structures under greater structural strain than broad ones of
comparable mass; large-bodied organisms carrying disproportionate
maintenance load for their size (already formalized as a scale effect on
maintenance cost, §7.5); rigid institutional hierarchies that fracture
under stress that flatter, more distributed organizations absorb. The
formal account below treats structural height as the dependent variable
in this relationship and informational alignment as the driver, rather
than treating the two as independent quantities that happen to
correlate.

\emph{\textbf{Axiom V1 (Verticality Is Rigid).}} These are named V1/V2,
distinct from Part 3's foundational Axiom 1/Axiom 2 and from the
collapse-layer's Axiom C1--C4 (§10.5b.15), to avoid colliding with
either --- the verticality-layer's own foundational claims, stated here
explicitly for the first time despite grounding material (h(t), V(t),
the pancaking Directional Alignment Effect of §8.8a.ix) that has
depended on them since RICD 10.3. A container under sufficient stress or
velocity concentrates its own energy into rigid, closed structure rather
than remaining open and distributed --- form, in this document's sense,
hardening as a direct consequence of pressure rather than as an
independent design choice.

\emph{\textbf{Axiom V2 (Horizontality Is Fluid).}} The complementary
claim: absent that concentrating pressure, a container's natural state
is open, distributed, uncompressed exchange --- function operating
freely across the container's own structure rather than being channeled
through a rigid form. V1 and V2 together are the reason h(t) is defined
as relaxing toward h\_target(I(t)) rather than being independently
fitted (Definition 10.5b.0.1 immediately below): rigidity is not a free
parameter, it is what V1 says happens under pressure, and fluidity is
what V2 says is recovered once that pressure lifts.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.0.1 (Structural Height and the
Verticality Operator).}}

Let a container C have a normalized structural height h(t) ∈ {[}h\_min,
1{]}, where h\_min \textgreater{} 0 is a strictly positive floor --- the
minimum structural coherence consistent with C remaining a bounded
container at all (§2.2, Axiom 1) --- and h(t) = 1 represents maximal,
closed, hierarchical rigidity. This is not a physical height in every
domain; it is a structural index of how rigid, closed, and
hierarchically constrained the container's boundary currently is.

Rather than treating h(t) as an independently fitted quantity, h(t) is
driven causally by informational alignment I(t) (§6.6) through a
relaxation dynamic:

d\emph{h}/dt = κ\_h · {[} h\_target(I(t)) − h(t) {]}, h\_target(I) =
h\_min + (1 − h\_min)·(1 − I)\^{}ξ,

with κ\_h \textgreater{} 0 a fitted relaxation rate and ξ ≥ 1 a fitted
exponent. Higher alignment sets a lower structural-height target; lower
alignment sets a higher one. Because h(t) chases h\_target(I(t)) rather
than jumping to it, structural relaxation necessarily lags behind and
follows alignment improvement --- it does not lead it, and cannot
produce a self-sustaining reduction in structural height by any
mechanism that intervenes on h(t) directly without I(t) itself
improving: a direct intervention can lower h(t) for as long as it
continues, but h(t) resumes relaxing toward h\_target(I(t)) the moment
that forcing lapses, per the same equation. The verticality operator is
then simply:

\emph{V}(t) := h(t) ∈ {[}h\_min, 1{]}.

This resolves what was previously an unspecified functional (Φ\_V,
monotonic in dh/dt, d²h/dt², S, δR, and Π\_f, with no computational
default given) by observing that once h(t) is itself derived from I(t)
--- which is already a function of S, δR, and Π\_f's downstream effects
on D\_op, D\_om, D\_pm through the dynamics of Part 6 --- a separate
wrapper functional adds complexity without adding information. The
rate-sensitivity the original formulation wanted (verticality responding
to how fast a container is rigidifying, not merely how rigid it
currently is) is preserved implicitly: h(t)'s own relaxation dynamic
already responds continuously to changes in I(t), so dh/dt is
recoverable directly from the equation above without a separate
operator.

\emph{\textbf{Corollary (Alignment Precedes Relaxation; a falsifiable
prediction).}}

Because h(t) chases h\_target(I(t)) with lag rather than tracking it
exactly, two directly testable predictions follow from the single
equation above, without further assumption. First, sustained improvement
in I(t) should measurably precede a corresponding decline in h(t), not
the reverse. Second, any intervention that forces h(t) below its
currently-implied target --- flattening structure without underlying
alignment actually improving --- predicts its own reversal: since dh/dt
= κ\_h·{[}h\_target(I(t)) − h(t){]} is positive whenever h(t) is below
target, the same dynamic that governs ordinary relaxation drives an
artificially-flattened container's structural height back up toward
h\_target(I(t)) once the intervention lapses. This is the formal
counterpart to the observation that forcing apparent flatness without
genuine alignment tends to produce rebound rigidity rather than lasting
change: it is not a separate claim requiring its own mechanism, but a
direct consequence of treating alignment as causally prior to structural
relaxation.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.0.2 (Horizontality Operator).}}

Horizontality is the dual of verticality, not an independently fitted
quantity:

\emph{𝒲}(t) := 1 − V(t) = 1 − h(t) ∈ {[}0, 1−h\_min{]}.

Because h\_min \textgreater{} 0, 𝒲(t) never reaches 1: a container never
achieves complete, unconstrained fluidity, consistent with some minimum
structural coherence being constitutive of remaining a bounded container
at all (Definition 10.5b.0.1). Maximal horizontality is 1 − h\_min ---
the semi-rigid matrix state --- not a fully flat, structureless field.

\textbf{Geometric support ratio.}

Height and rigidity alone do not determine structural durability. Two
containers with identical h(t) can differ sharply in how long that
structure persists, depending on how broadly the structure's boundary is
grounded relative to its vertical extension --- a broad-based structure
distributes load across a wide foundation; a narrow one concentrates it.
The same distinction separates a broad-based, widely distributed
structure from a narrow, concentrated one of comparable height, or a
broadly stakeholder-supported institution from a narrowly hierarchical
one of comparable size.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.0.3 (Geometric Support Ratio).}}

Define χ\_supp(C,t) ∈ (0,1{]}, a domain-specified measure of the breadth
of a container's structural or organizational base relative to its
vertical extension (adapter-supplied --- e.g.~base-contact area relative
to height for a physical structure, or breadth of distributed
stakeholder support relative to hierarchical depth for an institution).
χ\_supp = 1 represents maximal support for the container's current
height; values near 0 represent a narrow, precarious base relative to
height.

\textbf{Structural hazard and longevity.}

Rigidity beyond the minimum, and inadequate support for whatever
rigidity is present, both shorten a container's expected structural
lifespan. This is the formal content of the claim that vertical,
high-divergence structures burn themselves out faster than semi-rigid,
well-supported ones.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.0.4 (Structural Hazard Rate).}}

\emph{Ω}\_hazard(t) = κ\_Ω · V(t)\^{}ψ / χ\_supp(C,t),

where ψ ≥ 1 is a fitted exponent (ψ \textgreater{} 1 giving a
superlinear penalty for excess verticality, consistent with the
scale-dependence already established for maintenance cost in §7.5) and
κ\_Ω \textgreater{} 0 is a fitted rate coefficient. Hazard is minimized
--- but never driven to zero --- when V(t) = h\_min and χ\_supp = 1: the
semi-rigid matrix state, maximally supported, is the longest-lived
configuration a container can occupy, not an immortal one. Ω\_hazard(t)
\textgreater{} 0 always, since h\_min \textgreater{} 0; every container
remains subject to nonzero structural dissolution risk indefinitely,
consistent with no container being permanently exempt from Axiom 3's
nonzero cost of informational processing. Positive hazard alone does
not, by itself, guarantee eventual dissolution with certainty --- a
hazard rate that declines quickly enough can yield a finite cumulative
hazard and therefore a genuinely positive probability of indefinite
survival; the stronger claim of certain eventual dissolution
additionally requires the cumulative hazard to diverge, ∫₀\^{}∞
Ω\_hazard(t) dt = ∞, which this document does not assume by default and
which would need to be separately established or adopted for any domain
where it is wanted. Lower instantaneous hazard corresponds qualitatively
to greater expected longevity, all else equal; the exact expected
remaining lifetime depends on the future hazard trajectory, not merely
its instantaneous value at t, and requires a specified survival-function
model to compute precisely --- two containers with identical
Ω\_hazard(t\_0) can have very different expected remaining lifetimes if
one\textquotesingle s hazard falls rapidly afterward and the
other\textquotesingle s stays elevated. This document does not commit to
a specific survival-function form, and the qualitative, instantaneous
relationship above is what the diagnostic and comparative uses §10.5b
puts Ω\_hazard(t) to actually require, not a claim about expected
lifetime as a direct, computable function of instantaneous hazard alone.

\textbf{Axiom C3 (Verticality is Rigid).}

For any container C, as verticality V(t) increases beyond a
domain-specific threshold Θ\_V ∈ (h\_min, 1), the container undergoes a
phase transition from a semi-rigid matrix toward a closed-circuit
configuration, characterized by Π\_f(t) → 0 (feedback permeability
collapses), D\_op(t) → 1 (official-operational divergence maximizes),
d\_A(t) → 0 (adaptive capacity contracts), and R\_g(t) → 1 (rigidity
functional reaches maximum, §10.4).

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.0.1 (Verticality and the High-Entropy
Regime).}}

In the canonical, official-shielded high-entropy configuration ---
ordinary predatory or self-concealing high-entropy dynamics, the case
this document treats as default throughout --- a container is
characterized by V(t) \textgreater{} Θ\_V and D\_op(t) ≫ 0, with
correspondingly low Π\_f(t). Its elevated verticality is the structural
counterpart of its self-sustaining, extractive, and blind dynamics: the
official layer appears calm (σ\_o low) because rigid structure has
frozen the official messaging in place, while the operational layer
churns chaotically beneath it (σ\_p high). This conjunctive
characterization is not a universal defining property of the
high-entropy regime as such, which the regime classifier (§10.5b.1)
establishes independently through σ\_o/σ\_p alone: Definitions
10.5b.1.1--1.2's Type A and Type B full-alignment cases are genuinely
high-entropy by that classifier (operational is entropic, σ\_p high)
while carrying low D\_op, since official and operational have converged
--- accurately in Type A, falsely in Type B --- rather than remaining
divided. This proposition describes the ordinary, divided case; the
aligned exception is Definitions 10.5b.1.1--1.2's own subject and is not
additionally governed by the D\_op ≫ 0 clause here.

\textbf{Axiom C4 (Horizontality is Fluid).}

For any container C, horizontality W(t) is maximized (approaching, but
never reaching, 1 − h\_min) as Π\_f(t) → 1, I(t) → 1, D\_op(t) → 0, and
dR/dt \textgreater{} 0. In this state the container exhibits bounded
stress (S(t) \textless{} Θ\_S), high adaptive capacity (d\_A(t) ≈
d\_A\^{}max), low rigidity (R\_g(t) → 0), and short, bounded recovery
time (τ(t), §10.10).

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.0.2 (Horizontality and the Low-Entropy
Baseline).}}

A container in the low-entropy baseline regime (§10.5b.1) is
characterized by W(t) \textgreater{} Θ\_W and D\_op(t) ≈ 0, for some
threshold Θ\_W ∈ (0, 1−h\_min). Its elevated horizontality is the
structural expression of open, negentropic, self-aware systemic flow ---
the semi-rigid matrix state toward which recovery trajectories tend, per
Definition 10.5b.0.4, without ever reaching full structurelessness.

\textbf{The 90° inversion and tipping outcomes.}

Rather than an operator with an ambiguous, one-to-many output, the
relationship between a container's structural orientation and its
tipping outcome is best stated directly as a labeled transition, since
§10.5b.9's M\_tip thresholds already determine which outcome occurs ---
a separate ``inversion operator'' would only relabel that same
determination.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.0.5 (Structural Transition Labels).}}

A container beginning in a low-verticality configuration (V(t) ≈ h\_min,
a horizontal field) that undergoes a shock-driven phase transition
(Axiom C3) moves to a high-verticality configuration. From there,
resolution of a subsequent tipping event (§10.5b.9) is labeled:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  terminal lock (previously ``180° flip''): V(t) remains elevated; the
  \textgreater{} \textgreater{} container remains in a rigid, closed
  configuration and cannot \textgreater{} \textgreater{} return toward
  h\_min without external anchoring (§10.5b.13);
\item
  full reorganization (previously ``360° flip''): V(t) relaxes back
  \textgreater{} \textgreater{} toward h\_min via the ordinary
  alignment-driven dynamic of \textgreater{} \textgreater{} Definition
  10.5b.0.1, once I(t) recovers. This outcome is not \textgreater{}
  merely a favorable value within the high/medium-entropy \textgreater{}
  dichotomy that produced the tipping event in the first place ---
  \textgreater{} it is a genuine exit from that dichotomy entirely,
  landing the \textgreater{} container on the low-entropy baseline as a
  categorically \textgreater{} different mode, not a better-scoring
  position still defined by \textgreater{} the same two-regime framework
  it just left.
\end{itemize}

Which label applies is fully determined by the M\_tip-versus-threshold
comparison of §10.5b.9; the labels above are descriptive shorthand for
that outcome's structural consequence, not an independent mathematical
object.

\emph{\textbf{Corollary 10.5b.0.1 (Mass Weighting, Coherence, and
Structural Outcome).}}

In the high-entropy regime, a container's tipping outcome is governed by
the coherence factor κ(t) = 1/(1+D\_residual(t)) of §10.5b.8: residual
divergence outside the converging pair dissipates part of the driving
force as internal friction, but Flip Condition A structurally admits no
full-reorganization route under either of its causal sub-routes
(§10.5b.5), so the momentum that survives dissipation determines only
whether the terminal lock is reached at all (M\_tip ≥ M\_lock), never
which structural outcome results. In the medium-entropy regime, by
contrast, the outcome is governed by fluid-mass weighting (§10.5b.7):
because μ\_p \textgreater{} μ\_o structurally in this regime, the same
external launching force more readily crosses into the
full-reorganization range when the majority (operational) mass is what
converges.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.0.6 (Ultimate Convergence).}}

A container C undergoes Ultimate Convergence on an interval when:

\emph{V}(t) → h\_min, W(t) → 1−h\_min, B(t) → B\_open,

where B\_open is a boundary operator permitting maximal bidirectional
exchange consistent with remaining a bounded container, while membership
continuity is maintained throughout: χ(t,t′) ≥ χ\_min (Definition 9.3).
This is a thermodynamic transition from a high-hazard, closed
configuration to a low-hazard, open one (Definition 10.5b.0.4) --- not
dissolution, and not a transition to formlessness.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.0.7 (Identity Persistence, complete
criterion).}}

Combining Definition 9.3 (Part 9) with the semi-rigidity floor
established here: a container C maintains full identity across {[}t′,
t{]} if and only if both (a) χ(t,t′) ≥ χ\_min (membership continuity is
maintained) and (b) χ\_supp(C,s) ≥ χ\_supp,min for all s ∈ {[}t′,t{]}
(§10.5b.0's geometric support ratio, structural base breadth relative to
height, remains above its own admissibility floor throughout) --- i.e.,
structural change alone, however large in V(t) itself, does not end
identity provided both membership continuity (a) and a genuinely
non-collapsing support base (b) hold throughout. An earlier version of
condition (b) stated V(s) ≤ 1 for all s; this added no independent
content, since V(t) is defined as h(t) ∈ {[}h\_min, 1{]} (Definition
10.5b.0.1) and is therefore bounded above by 1 for every admissible
container as a matter of definition, not as a condition any real
trajectory could fail. χ\_supp remaining bounded away from collapse is a
genuinely independent, sometimes-false condition instead: a container
can satisfy membership continuity while its structural base narrows
toward a single point of failure (Ω\_hazard's own κ\_Ω·V\^{}ψ/χ\_supp
form already shows why this matters --- hazard diverges as χ\_supp → 0
regardless of what V(t) is doing), and this is precisely the loss of
bounded-container structure the criterion needs an independent condition
to rule out. Ultimate Convergence (Definition 10.5b.0.6) is the case
where both conditions hold while V(t) trends toward its floor: identity
persists, hazard falls, longevity increases. Dissolution is the case
where (a) fails --- χ(t,t′) falls below χ\_min because membership has
scattered into unrelated clusters --- regardless of what V(t) is doing
at the time; a container can dissolve while structurally rigid
(fragmentation under sustained high hazard) or, in principle, while
structurally relaxed (gradual absorption into a neighboring cluster).
The two failure modes are structurally distinct even though both end
identity, and §8.8a.v's ``complete fragmentation'' outcome and §8.8a's
locked-node dynamics are instances of the former.

\textbf{Relationship to the entropy regime classification.}

Verticality/horizontality (this subsection) and the entropy regime
classification (§10.5b.1, via σ\_o, σ\_p) answer different questions and
should not be expected to jointly triangulate one another from a single
V/W reading. V(t) measures overall structural rigidity --- a property
shared, to varying degree, by both non-baseline regimes, since both
arise from the same shock-branching mechanism (§10.5b.2) departing from
the same baseline. σ\_o and σ\_p measure something orthogonal: which
layer, official or operational, is carrying the apparent volatility. A
high-entropy and a medium-entropy container can therefore present with
comparable V(t) --- both departures from baseline elevate structural
rigidity relative to the semi-rigid matrix floor --- while being sharply
distinguished by σ\_o versus σ\_p, which is precisely why §10.5b.1 uses
the variance signature, not V(t), as the classification criterion.
V(t)'s role is to explain why departure from baseline carries a hazard
consequence (Definition 10.5b.0.4), not to re-derive which departure a
container has undergone.

\hypertarget{b.0.8-sustained-horizontal-matrix}{%
\subsection{10.5b.0.8 Sustained Horizontal
Matrix}\label{b.0.8-sustained-horizontal-matrix}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.0.8 (Sustained Horizontal Matrix).}}

A container C undergoing Ultimate Convergence (Definition 10.5b.0.6) is
sustained on an interval {[}t′, t{]} when the convergence conditions ---
V(t) = h\_min, W(t) = 1−h\_min, B(t) = B\_open, χ(t,t′) ≥ χ\_min ---
hold throughout the interval rather than only momentarily. Where
sustained, C acquires one further property beyond Definition 10.5b.0.6's
own content: immunity, not merely resilience, to tipping-momentum
accumulation. For any incoming Drive\_B(j,t) or propagated kick
K\_\{j→k\}(t) (§8.8a, §8.8b) directed at C during the sustained
interval, C's own contribution to M\_tip is forced to zero, regardless
of the striking force's magnitude. This is a difference in kind from
Z(t)'s resilience (§10.5b.9a), which lowers the cost of a striking event
that still occurs; a sustained container's striking events do not occur
in the sense of accumulating momentum at all --- there is, as this
document's own imagery for the state has it, nothing there to rock.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.0.3 (Stress Non-Accumulation, Already
Entailed).}}
