so that higher Z always shortens the excursion. Cost follows the same
underlying direction --- higher resilience lowers cost --- in both
cases, stated in the surface language natural to each direction (rising
Z lowering cost for the healing case; falling Z raising cost for the
collapsing case, which is the identical relationship read the other way,
not an opposite one):

\emph{c}\_n = c\_0 · (1 − Z(t\_n)) · {[}1 + κ\_backslide · (1 − Z(t\_n))
· 𝟙(s(t) = −1){]}, κ\_backslide ≥ 0 fitted (the linear base dependence
on Z is a computational default consistent with the theoretical
direction --- cost falls as resilience rises, in both cases --- but is
not itself forced by that direction; alternative curves sharing the same
monotonic relationship remain admissible),

so that for s = +1 (healing), rising Z drives both cost and duration
down, with κ\_backslide contributing nothing; for s = −1 (collapsing),
falling Z drives cost up along the same base relationship, further
amplified by the depletion penalty κ\_backslide · (1 − Z(t\_n))
specifically as resilience runs low, while duration correspondingly
lengthens per τ\_n = τ\_0·(1 − Z(t\_n)) above (the excursion is both
longer and, at low Z particularly, more damaging per unit time than the
healing direction's base relationship alone would give --- a
depleted-resilience trajectory takes both more and longer, not a
shorter, sharper penalty). κ\_backslide = 0 recovers the plain shared
relationship with no directional asymmetry at all, which is a legitimate
modeling choice if no real, measured difference in severity between
healing and collapsing excursions is found. For the high-entropy
self-healing case, cost is given directly by the generalized F\_conf of
§10.5b.3:

\emph{c}\_n\^{}(high) = F\_conf(t\_n) = ζ · Π\_f(t\_n) · D\_op(t\_n).

Resilience also feeds back into the feedback operator's own drift rate
for a medium-entropy healing container, making correction itself more
effective as resilience accumulates:

\emph{γ}\_ℱ(t) = γ\_ℱ,0 · (1 + κ\_Z · Z(t)), κ\_Z \textgreater{} 0
fitted, applied in §6.4's stochastic flow for this container.

\hypertarget{b.9b-pre-breach-self-rescue-and-the-counterweight-window}{%
\subsection{10.5b.9b Pre-Breach Self-Rescue and the Counterweight
Window}\label{b.9b-pre-breach-self-rescue-and-the-counterweight-window}}

§10.5b.9's thresholds govern what happens once a tipping event is
already underway. Before that point, a high-entropy container that
recognizes its own trajectory early enough has one further,
self-generated option not available afterward: engaging the same D\_om →
0 convergence event that, post-tip, contributes to A2's terminal lock
(§10.5b.5, §10.5b.8) --- but engaging it pre-tip, as a counterweight
rather than a resolution.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.9b.1 (Counterweight Convergence).}}

For a high-entropy container with hull-breach margin margin(t) =
θ\_hull·Π\_f(t) − {[}(1−I(t))+S̃(t){]} (§10.5a, §16.3) not yet negative,
define counterweight engagement as a self-generated convergence pulling
D\_om → 0 --- official and meta layers aligning with each other, on the
side opposite the operational layer's pull --- while D\_op, D\_pm remain
at their current levels. This raises I(t) directly (§6.6), and therefore
raises margin(t).

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.9b.1 (Three Zones).}}

Let dI/dt\textbar\_counter denote the rate of integrity increase
produced by counterweight engagement.

\begin{itemize}
\item
  margin(t) \textgreater{} 0: if dI/dt\textbar\_counter is sufficient to
  keep margin(t) \textgreater{} \textgreater{} ≥ 0 for the duration of
  engagement, the container returns to \textgreater{} \textgreater{}
  bounded oscillation within its current regime (§6.9) --- the
  \textgreater{} \textgreater{} tipping event is avoided entirely, not
  merely delayed.
\item
  margin(t) = 0 exactly: a knife-edge case. If dI/dt\textbar\_counter
  \textgreater{} \textgreater{} exactly offsets the ongoing decline, the
  container holds at the \textgreater{} \textgreater{} threshold rather
  than tipping or receding --- an inherently \textgreater{}
  \textgreater{} unstable stall, sustained only as long as the
  counterweight itself \textgreater{} \textgreater{} is sustained.
\item
  margin(t) \textless{} 0 (past hull-breach): counterweight engagement
  can no \textgreater{} \textgreater{} longer prevent the tipping event.
  Per Corollary 10.5b.0.1, Flip \textgreater{} \textgreater{} Condition
  A structurally admits no route back to bounded \textgreater{}
  \textgreater{} oscillation once tipping is underway. From this point,
  D\_om → 0 \textgreater{} \textgreater{} convergence still matters, but
  only as one of Flip Condition A's \textgreater{} \textgreater{} two
  ordinary post-tip resolution routes (§10.5b.5's A2), not as
  \textgreater{} a \textgreater{} prevention mechanism.
\end{itemize}

This gives a high-entropy container exactly one self-generated escape
route, with a real, bounded window of effectiveness rather than an
open-ended one --- consistent with §10.5b.10's observation that this
regime's tipping is comparatively invisible in advance: the window
exists, but a container (or an outside observer) has to recognize the
approach early enough to use it.

\hypertarget{b.9c-deliberate-redirection-during-frantic-striking}{%
\subsection{10.5b.9c Deliberate Redirection During Frantic
Striking}\label{b.9c-deliberate-redirection-during-frantic-striking}}

Where a container recognizes its situation with enough lead time to act,
but too late for §10.5b.9b's counterweight window (margin(t) already
negative, or dI/dt\textbar\_counter insufficient to hold it), it may
retain one further degree of freedom: choosing the direction of its
discharge rather than letting §8.8a.ii's vector sum ⃗Λ\_i(t) default to
an indiscriminate multi-directional pattern.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.9c.1 (Targeted Redirection).}}

Rather than ⃗Λ\_i(t) = Σ\_\{j∈N\_i(t)\} Λ\_ij(t)·û\_ij(t) summing
indiscriminately over all coupled neighbors, a deliberately-redirecting
container concentrates its forcing onto a chosen subset
N\_i\^{}target(t) ⊆ N\_i(t) --- typically a single neighbor ---
reweighting which neighbors receive the forcing without necessarily
changing its total magnitude. The choice is usually made in the hope of
steadying against that specific neighbor, or of avoiding a different
one.

This does not guarantee the outcome sought. A targeted neighbor may
still resolve through ordinary cascade dynamics (§8.8a.iii's
medium-entropy launch) rather than providing the stabilization hoped
for, and deliberate targeting of an otherwise-unlikely neighbor is one
of the routes formalized in §10.5b.11b below.

Preventing a tipping event outright is preferable to managing its
aftermath for both regimes, but the two regimes differ sharply in how
foreseeable the event is. A medium-entropy (medium-entropy) container's
impending tipping event is comparatively visible in advance --- its
official layer is, by regime definition, the visibly chaotic one,
directly observable as σ\_o(t) rising. Because its outcome genuinely
could go either way (B1's lock or B2's reorganization), prevention ---
steadying the container before it tips at all --- is substantially safer
than letting the event occur. A high-entropy (high-entropy) container's
impending tip is, by contrast, comparatively invisible in advance,
precisely because its official layer presents as stable while the actual
instability is hidden in the operational layer; this is why
post-collapse intervention receives comparatively more emphasis for this
regime in practice.

\hypertarget{b.9d-pre-flip-disintegration}{%
\subsection{10.5b.9d Pre-Flip
Disintegration}\label{b.9d-pre-flip-disintegration}}

Per §8.5a's coupling-valence classification, a target crossing from
ongoing extraction (§8.6a's chronic parasitism) into this section's
disintegration, or into a full tipping cascade, is the acute case of the
same (+/-) sign pair --- predation rather than parasitism, marked by the
target's own collapse resolution rather than continued, weakened
functioning under an ongoing coupling.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.9d.1 (Multi-Source Chaos Pressure).}}

For container j targeted simultaneously by multiple high-entropy
neighbors, define accumulated chaos pressure as the trailing integral of
§8.8a's existing multi-source launch drive,

D\_chaos(j,t) = ∫\_\{t−w\}\^{}\{t\} Drive\_B(j,s) ds,

over a fitted trailing window w, distinguishing sustained multi-source
exposure from a single transient peak that Drive\_B(j,t) alone would
already capture instantaneously.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.9d.2 (Disintegration Condition).}}

Where D\_chaos(j,t) exceeds a threshold scaled by j's own internal
cohesion (§9.10.1's Λ\_int(j,t)),

D\_chaos(j,t) \textgreater{} Θ\_disintegrate · Λ\_int(j,t),
Θ\_disintegrate \textgreater{} 0 fitted,

while j's own tipping-momentum resolution (M̃\_tip(j,t), §10.5b.9) has
not yet reached any of Definition 10.5b.0.5's labeled outcomes, and
χ(t,t') (Definition 9.2) falls below χ\_min during this same window as a
direct consequence of the pressure rather than of voluntary membership
change --- j disintegrates. Its boundary operator B(t) transitions to an
undefined state, distinct from both B\_open (Ultimate Convergence,
Definition 10.5b.0.6) and the closed configuration of terminal lock: j
ceases to be a container in the sense Definition 2.2 requires, rather
than settling into either of §10.5b.0.5's labeled configurations.

Scaling the threshold by Λ\_int(j,t) rather than treating
Θ\_disintegrate as an absolute constant gives the condition a principled
form: a more internally cohesive container can absorb proportionally
greater simultaneous pressure before disintegrating, consistent with
§9.10.1's account of cohesion as the sole containment mechanism once
vertical structure is minimal --- disintegration is what happens when
external pressure exceeds what a container's own cohesion, whatever it
currently is, can hold together.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.9d.1 (Disintegration Is Distinct From
Isolation Dissolution).}}

§8.8a's existing dissolution and Definition 10.5b.9d.2's disintegration
are opposite in mechanism and should not be conflated despite both
resulting in a container ceasing to exist as such. §8.8a's case arises
from too few remaining coupled neighbors --- a network fragmenting until
no further launch is possible. This case arises from too many
simultaneous pressuring neighbors --- overload rather than abandonment.
A network-level audit encountering a dissolved container should check
which condition obtains before attributing the outcome to isolation by
default: disintegration under Definition 10.5b.9d.2 predicts a preceding
period of elevated D\_chaos(j,t) from multiple concurrent sources, while
§8.8a's dissolution predicts a preceding decline in λ₂(L(t)) and
shrinking coupled-neighbor count --- empirically distinguishable
signatures for what would otherwise be recorded as the same
undifferentiated outcome.

\textbf{10.5b.10 Steadying vs.~One-Time Reset}

A medium-entropy container that completes a B2 reorganization, or is
steadied pre-flip, benefits from external anchoring primarily as a speed
multiplier. A high-entropy container is structurally different: merely
righting or resetting it without addressing internal composition is
insufficient, since its unaltered internal mechanics (F\_i, still driven
by whatever unresolved D\_op and unvented heat remain) will trigger
renewed violent oscillation almost immediately. What this regime
requires is sustained steadying --- continuous external containment
disallowing both further oscillation and further extraction/displacement
(§8.6a) --- maintained over time, not applied once. Sustained steadying
is also the mechanism by which a high-entropy container's composition
can gradually shift toward the medium-entropy or low-entropy baseline
regimes, per Axiom C1: with extraction and oscillation both disallowed
for long enough, the feedback operator loses the external support it
needs to sustain convergence around the corrupted signal, and drifts
toward the true one instead.

\hypertarget{b.11a-post-breach-redirection-versus-resistance}{%
\subsection{10.5b.11a Post-Breach Redirection versus
Resistance}\label{b.11a-post-breach-redirection-versus-resistance}}

Once a container has crossed hull-breach (§10.5a) and entered runaway
acceleration (Axiom C2), the character of external intervention needed
reverses from what §10.5b.10's prevention guidance recommends. Before
hull-breach, opposing an oscillation's phase is damping and protective
(§10.5b.6). After hull-breach, the same opposing intervention is
destructive: momentum that has already accumulated cannot be canceled,
only redirected or blocked, and blocking forces a violent, concentrated
discharge rather than preventing one.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.11a.1 (Redirection Channel).}}

At the moment a container's tipping momentum M\_tip(t) is discharging
(§8.8b), define a redirection channel availability Γ\_redir(t) ∈
{[}0,1{]}: the degree to which an external structure --- an anchor, an
aligned momentum source, or a coupled neighbor --- is positioned to
carry the discharging momentum through a full rotation rather than
absorb or oppose it. Γ\_redir(t) = 0 indicates no such structure is
available or engaged; Γ\_redir(t) = 1 indicates full redirection
capacity.

\emph{\textbf{Computational default.}} Where no domain-specific
composition is supplied, Γ\_redir(t) = max(Γ\_anchor(t), Γ\_neighbor(t),
Γ\_intrinsic(t)), the largest of: anchor-supplied availability
(Anchor(k→C,t), §10.5b.2.6, where present), the strongest
currently-aligned coupled neighbor's own capacity to receive redirected
momentum, and the intrinsic fluid-mass-derived channel already given for
Flip Condition B below. Taking the maximum rather than a sum or average
reflects that a single sufficient channel, not the accumulation of
several partial ones, is what carries a full rotation; this default is
adapter-overridable where a domain-specific composition is better
justified.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.11a.1 (Discharge Character
Generalized).}}

Lemma 8.8b.1's impulsive-versus-dissipative discharge split, previously
determined by which outcome (B1 or B2) a container reached, is a special
case of a more general rule: discharge is impulsive when Γ\_redir(t)
\textless{} Θ\_redir during the tipping window, and dissipative when
Γ\_redir(t) ≥ Θ\_redir, Θ\_redir ∈ (0,1) fitted or normalized to the
same scale as this document\textquotesingle s other outcome thresholds
--- an explicit, evaluable threshold replacing the earlier qualitative
``sufficiently high,'' regardless of route. For Flip Condition B,
fluid-mass weighting (§10.5b.7) is what determines Γ\_redir
intrinsically --- μ\_p \textgreater{} μ\_o gives B2 an inherent channel
B1 lacks. For Flip Condition A, no route supplies Γ\_redir intrinsically
(Corollary 10.5b.0.1 holds unmodified for the unaided case); a nonzero
Γ\_redir must be supplied externally.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.11a.2 (Resistance Blocks the Channel).}}

An intervention that opposes momentum during active discharge (t near
t\_tip, §8.8b) rather than redirecting it drives Γ\_redir(t) → 0
regardless of what Γ\_redir would otherwise have been, forcing the
impulsive discharge form even where a channel was structurally
available. This is the formal content of bracing being harmful
specifically after hull-breach: resistance does not reduce M\_tip(t), it
removes the channel through which M\_tip(t) could otherwise discharge
gradually.

\textbf{Two of three routes to nonzero Γ\_redir for Flip Condition A
(the third, involving a second struggling container, is developed
separately in §10.5b.11b).}

\textbf{1. External anchor (A1 or A2).} An anchor engaged during the
discharge window (§10.5b.13's eligibility conditions apply) supplies
Γ\_redir directly, converting the container's governing dynamics for
that window from F\_i-dominated to effectively Σ\_jΛ\_ij-dominated ---
lending the container Flip B's full-rotation option for the duration of
the intervention. This is the general-purpose route and the only one
available to a purely self-propelled (A1) container, which has no
external component to redirect in the first place.

\textbf{2. Aligned external knock (A2 only).} Where the tipping event is
already externally triggered (A2's own definition) and the knock's
direction aligns with D\_om → 0 convergence, the knock itself supplies
Γ\_redir without requiring a separately engaged anchor. This route is
unavailable to A1, which by definition has no external strike to align.

\emph{\textbf{Corollary 10.5b.11a.1 (Stripped Reorganization).}}

Route 2 reaches the same M̃\_tip ≥ M\_c threshold band as B2 (§10.5b.9),
but the resulting reorganization is not B2's organic convergence around
a validated operational signal. Because the redirecting force was an
external strike rather than the container's own accurately-monitored
operational layer, the reset carries the operational layer to a null or
rebuilt state rather than recovering it --- a full rotation is
completed, S(t) and δR(t) reset, but the container's operational history
is discarded rather than preserved. This is a survivable, non-terminal
outcome, and a costly one, distinct from both terminal lock and clean
reorganization.

A third route --- mutual coupling with another struggling container ---
also supplies Γ\_redir, but involves enough additional structure
(predatory targeting, accidental capture, and voluntary sacrifice are
all distinct sub-cases) to warrant its own subsection: see §10.5b.11b.

Falsifiable prediction. Consistent with Axiom C2's evidentiary framing:
containers with an engaged redirection channel during discharge should
show measurably longer, lower-magnitude discharge signatures
(dissipative-form K\_j(t)) and higher post-event survival/reorganization
rates than matched containers whose channel is blocked or absent during
the same discharge window, holding M\_tip(t) itself constant.

\hypertarget{b.11b-mutual-coupling-under-collapse-predation-accident-and-sacrifice}{%
\subsection{10.5b.11b Mutual Coupling Under Collapse: Predation,
Accident, and
Sacrifice}\label{b.11b-mutual-coupling-under-collapse-predation-accident-and-sacrifice}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.11b.1 (Discharge Alignment Tolerance).}}

Two containers' discharges qualify as coupled --- for any of the
sub-cases below --- if \textbar t\_tip,A − t\_tip,B\textbar{}
\textless{} ε\_align (a fitted temporal tolerance) and their forcing
direction vectors satisfy û\_A·û\_B \textgreater{} cos(θ\_align) for a
fitted angular tolerance θ\_align. This gives every sub-case a genuine,
nonzero-probability basin rather than requiring exact coincidence.

\textbf{i. Predatory Targeting (Deliberate).}

A high-entropy container approaching terminal lock (M̃\_tip(i)
approaching the M\_lock/M\_c band, §10.5b.9) may extend its targeting
beyond its ordinary extraction pattern --- §8.6a's ρ(j), which
structurally favors medium-entropy neighbors and disfavors baseline ones
--- to include an otherwise-untargeted low-entropy baseline neighbor,
using that neighbor's structure as a landing surface for its own
discharge. Two mechanisms, both operative and not mutually exclusive:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Gradual aperture widening. ρ(j) gains an explicit dependence on the
  \textgreater{} \textgreater{} extracting container's own proximity to
  terminal lock: ρ(j;i) = \textgreater{} \textgreater{}
  1/\{1+exp{[}−(ρ\_0(j) + κ\_desp·F̄\_i(t)){]}\} (§8.6a.1's corrected,
  \textgreater{} \textgreater{} bounded form; F̄\_i(t) =
  1−exp(−λ\_F·ℱ\_i(t))) --- as \textgreater{} \textgreater{} i's own
  accumulated unvented heat rises, its targeting \textgreater{}
  \textgreater{} discrimination continuously loosens to include
  neighbors it would \textgreater{} \textgreater{} ordinarily ignore.
\item
  Switched pathway. Independent of the above, once i crosses into the
  \textgreater{} \textgreater{} terminal-lock band, a separate targeting
  mode --- off below the \textgreater{} \textgreater{} band, on above it
  --- activates, reflecting a genuinely \textgreater{} different
  \textgreater{} behavioral mode rather than a continuation of
  \textgreater{} ordinary extraction.
\end{itemize}

Which mechanism dominates in a given case is an empirical question this
document does not resolve in advance. The targeted baseline neighbor
typically resolves to terminal lock rather than reorganization, since it
did not choose or prepare for the role (Corollary 10.5b.11a.1's
stripped-reorganization logic applies, but the predator's resulting
reorganization is generally a lesser version of it than an aligned-knock
route would produce, since baseline containers hold no accumulated
momentum of their own to lend --- this asymmetry needs empirical
calibration, not asserted as a specific ratio here).

\textbf{ii. Accidental Capture (Cascading Vortex).}

Driven by declining algebraic connectivity (λ₂(L(t)) → 0, §8.8a.v)
during a wider cascading failure, a baseline container can be drawn into
a discharge window purely through proximity --- via its coupling weight
W\_ij(t) rather than through deliberate targeting (i, above) or
fluid-mass alignment (§10.5b.7). Two branches, both real and not
reducible to one another:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Toward a failing container. The baseline container is pulled toward
  \textgreater{} \textgreater{} a container resolving to terminal lock;
  per Corollary \textgreater{} \textgreater{} 10.5b.11a.1's logic it
  typically locks alongside it, though \textgreater{} \textgreater{}
  survival is possible depending on its own mass or resource
  \textgreater{} \textgreater{} relative to the pulling container's
  discharge magnitude, and on \textgreater{} \textgreater{} the phase of
  the cycle at which it was captured. This document \textgreater{}
  \textgreater{} does not commit to a closed-form survival probability,
  flagging it \textgreater{} \textgreater{} as a further
  empirically-open item alongside those in §14.2.
\item
  Toward a reorganizing container. The baseline container is pulled
  \textgreater{} \textgreater{} toward a container resolving to full
  reorganization instead; here \textgreater{} \textgreater{} it can
  passively benefit from proximity to a successful reset \textgreater{}
  \textgreater{} rather than being harmed by it, consistent with
  Γ\_redir(t) \textgreater{} being \textgreater{} high, by construction,
  in this branch.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{iii. Sacrificial Lifeboat.}

Distinct from (i) and (ii): two comparably-struggling containers,
neither predatory nor accidentally swept in, where the discharge
asymmetry favors one's survival through the other's own trajectory
redirection. Decompose per §16.8's Definition 16.7 into a
strength-explained component and a remainder:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Logic-based. The strength-explained component alone accounts for the
  \textgreater{} \textgreater{} asymmetry --- the comparatively weaker
  container at the time \textgreater{} ends \textgreater{} up absorbing
  the discharge, following directly from the \textgreater{} same
  \textgreater{} comparison already used elsewhere in this document
  \textgreater{} (§10.5b.7's \textgreater{} mass-weighting logic, here
  applied between peers \textgreater{} rather than \textgreater{} within
  one container's layers).
\item
  Intention-based. A genuine remainder exists beyond the strength
  \textgreater{} \textgreater{} comparison --- per §16.8's Hypothesis,
  attributable to the \textgreater{} \textgreater{} sacrificing
  container's own pre-event messaging history, findable \textgreater{}
  \textgreater{} where confirmable via §16.7's backward-facing
  procedure, not \textgreater{} \textgreater{} derivable from either
  container's state at the moment of collision \textgreater{}
  \textgreater{} alone.
\end{itemize}

\emph{\textbf{A second, distinct discovery route for the intention-based
remainder.}} §16.7's messaging-history procedure is not the only way to
find the actual reason behind a specific instance of intention-based
sacrifice, and need not be treated as the default first resort. Where
the sacrificing container is itself recursively composed (§5's
child-container structure), an internal audit of its own child systems
--- their individual states, couplings, and resource allocations at the
moment of the sacrifice --- can surface the actual reason directly,
rather than inferring it backward from external messaging history alone:
a specific child system's own condition may be what the parent container
was actually protecting, in a way no record of the parent's own external
messaging would show. Both routes are legitimate and can be used
together; neither supersedes the other, and where they disagree, the
more direct evidentiary source (a child system's own confirmed state at
the time) should be weighted over the inferential one (reconstructed
messaging history), consistent with this document's general preference
for direct attestation over inference wherever both are available
(§10.5b.18.4a).

A further, specific version of the intention-based case: a container
facing terminal lock either way may choose which neighbor absorbs its
discharge, or choose to absorb a neighbor's discharge itself,
specifically to keep a wider cascade (§8.8a.v) from propagating further
--- self-sacrifice undertaken to limit collateral damage rather than for
reasons reducible to the strength comparison alone. This is a specific
instance of the intention-based remainder, not a distinct mechanism, and
is subject to the same §16.8 confirmation standard: it should be
reported as a hypothesis unless the sacrificing container's pre-event
record independently supports it.

\textbf{iv. Predatory Lifeboat.}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.11b.4 (Predatory Lifeboat).}} A container
i approaching Flip Condition A's terminal-lock threshold, with no
external anchor available, may launch a frantic strike (§8.8a) targeted
specifically at a low-entropy or otherwise healthy neighbor k, using k
as an involuntary redirection target:

M\_tip(i,t) recomputed against k's absorbed momentum, per §10.5b.11a's
redirection mechanics, but with the redirection channel Γ\_redir(t)
supplied coercively by i's own extraction (§8.6a) rather than
voluntarily by an anchor. This lets i reach the same non-terminal,
stripped-reorganization band §10.5b.11a describes, at k's direct
expense: k, lacking i's accumulated coherence advantage and not a
willing anchor, absorbs the discharge as a full terminal lock of its own
rather than a survivable redirection --- i tumbles into stripped
reorganization while k freezes. This is a real, distinct failure mode
external anchor-eligibility screening (§10.5b.13) exists specifically to
prevent: an eligible anchor's own designed capacity and voluntary
participation are what turn redirection into rescue rather than
sacrifice: an unscreened neighbor pressed into this role has neither.

\textbf{v. Cascading Vortex.}

Definition 10.5b.11b.5 (Cascading Vortex). A baseline container m, not
coupled to the collapsing container i and not a target of any extraction
or redirection, can still be drawn into i's tipping event purely through
network proximity: i's discharge/venting flux (H\_i, §8.6a --- renamed
here from ``unvented heat,'' which is now ℐ\_i\textquotesingle s own
role, not H\_i\textquotesingle s, since §8.8a.1 established H\_i as the
instantaneous venting rate rather than accumulated pressure) discharges
multi-directionally as it approaches terminal lock, and any container
within m's own coupling range (Λ\_im(t) \textgreater{} 0, even weakly)
absorbs a real momentum contribution proportional to that proximity ---
this is a live-discharge mechanism, driven by i\textquotesingle s
current venting flux as it actively discharges, not by accumulated
trapped pressure, and a real, specific route into this mechanism now
exists beyond ordinary approach-to-terminal-lock: the
Resolution-Kick-driven frantic striking described above (§8.8a)
discharges at whatever coupled neighbors N\_i(t) currently exist,
without regard to whether they had any involvement in the original
extraction --- a predator destabilized by a decoupling
victim\textquotesingle s own accurate kick can, in striking out to
soothe that destabilization, draw a previously-uninvolved baseline
neighbor into the vortex the same way sustained approach to terminal
lock would. The grid-level consequence is a real, indirect chain worth
naming explicitly: a single parasitized victim\textquotesingle s own
successful resolution can propagate instability well beyond its own
predator, into containers that never had any coupling to either the
victim or the original extraction at all, entirely through the
predator\textquotesingle s own destabilized lashing-out rather than
through any further extraction on the predator\textquotesingle s part.

M\_tip(m,t) ← M\_tip(m,t) + κ\_vortex·Λ\_im(t)·H\_i(t), κ\_vortex
\textgreater{} 0 fitted --- an additive update, not set inclusion, which
was never a meaningful operator for a scalar momentum quantity and is
corrected here,

added to m's own existing tipping-momentum terms. No targeting
mechanism, predatory or otherwise, need be active for this term to be
nonzero --- it is a structural consequence of coupling proximity to an
active discharge event, not a choice by i, m, or anything between them.
This supplies the previously missing forcing pathway by which the
cascade can reach otherwise-uninvolved baseline containers, rather than
remaining confined to i's already-coupled predatory or sacrificial
neighbors; whether the induced momentum actually drives m toward
tipping, and whether enough such inductions actually produce λ₂(L(t)) →
0 (Definition 8.8a.4) at the network level, is determined by m's own
downstream tipping dynamics and the network\textquotesingle s aggregate
response, not established by this term alone.

\emph{\textbf{Refinement to purpose-built anchors (§10.5b.13's
designed-capacity treatment).}} An anchor's safety was established there
as a static design property --- eligible up to its designed capacity, an
ordinary coupled neighbor beyond it. A real, additional dynamic behavior
belongs alongside that static property, not replacing it: an eligible
purpose-built anchor does not merely have sufficient capacity, it
actively tracks the anchored container's own trajectory while absorbing
discharge, and its own parameters (a\_i, e\_i) reset toward baseline
once the discharge is fully absorbed, rather than accumulating
unresolved strain the way an ordinary coupled neighbor's would. This is
what distinguishes a genuinely engineered shield from a merely
high-capacity neighbor that happens not to have failed yet.

\hypertarget{b.11c-active-search-search-suppression-and-mirror-threat-avoidance}{%
\subsection{10.5b.11c Active Search, Search Suppression, and
Mirror-Threat
Avoidance}\label{b.11c-active-search-search-suppression-and-mirror-threat-avoidance}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.11c.1 (Active Search Intensity).}}

For a high-entropy container i, define Σ\_search(i,t) ≥ 0, the rate at
which i initiates evaluation of candidate coupling targets beyond its
existing edges --- distinct from ρ(j), which governs persistence of
couplings already formed. Where i satisfies §10.5b.18.25's structural
extraction dependence, Σ\_search(i,t) scales with i's own deficit:

Σ\_search(i,t) = κ\_search · max(0, M\_maint(C,t) + M\_decep(t) −
P\_prod(t)), κ\_search \textgreater{} 0 fitted,

so that search intensity tracks the severity of i's own unmet need,
rather than being a free-floating behavioral parameter. A container not
satisfying structural extraction dependence has Σ\_search(i,t) = 0 by
this default, though an adapter may supply a nonzero value directly
where a domain has independent evidence of active-seeking behavior
absent formal dependence.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.11c.1a (Target Selection Among
Candidates).}} Σ\_search(i,t) governs how intensely i evaluates
candidates beyond its existing edges; it does not by itself determine
which candidate is actually selected where several are being evaluated
at once. Selection among candidates \{C\_k\} is jointly determined by
proximity and target-type compatibility, reusing machinery already built
rather than introducing new primitives:

Select(i,t) = argmax\_k {[} w\_prox · Prox(i,k,t) + w\_type ·
𝒱\_\{i→k\}(t) {]}, w\_prox + w\_type = 1, both fitted,

where Prox(i,k,t) is i's positional proximity to candidate k (derived
from existing network distance or positional embedding, §8.6a.7, where
available) and 𝒱\_\{i→k\}(t) (§8.6a.4) is i's own observation-mapped
estimate of k's vulnerability, not k's true latent state. This is
distinct from Definition 8.3.1's first-contact attraction, which governs
general network re-entry toward cohesive-looking candidates for any
newly-uncoupled container; this definition governs specifically which
extraction candidate a high-entropy container under active search
selects, driven by exploitability rather than cohesion. During a frantic
striking phase (§8.8a.2) specifically, w\_prox rises relative to its
ordinary fitted value --- accelerating divergence narrows deliberation
and favors whichever compatible candidate is nearest, consistent with
this document's own account of frantic striking as thermodynamic
discharge rather than strategic search.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.11c.2 (Search Suppression by Ambient
Cohesion).}}

Active search does not operate at full strength against an arbitrary
population; its effective reach is degraded by the ambient cohesion
density of the containers it searches among. Define

Σ\_search\^{}eff(i,t) = Σ\_search(i,t) · exp{[}−κ\_amb · Λ\_amb(t){]},

where Λ\_amb(t) is the resource-weighted mean of §9.10.1's
Λ\_int(C\_k,t) across all candidates \{C\_k\} within i's observable
search range, and κ\_amb \textgreater{} 0 is fitted. A search population
that is, on average, densely coupled among itself suppresses i's
effective search reach exponentially rather than linearly --- consistent
with the document's own imagery for this mechanism, in which a
population's mutual coupling does not merely fail to attract a predator
(§8.3.1's Corollary 8.3.1.1 already covers that passive case) but
actively degrades an already-searching predator's ability to locate a
target at all.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.11c.1 (Grid-Level Predator Starvation,
Search Channel).}}

Where Λ\_amb(t) rises across a network --- driven by the same
cohesion-building dynamics §9.10.1 and §8.3.1 already establish ---
every structurally extraction-dependent container's
Σ\_search\^{}eff(i,t) falls in tandem, independent of and additional to
§8.3.1's passive-attraction channel. The two channels close different
routes to the same target: Corollary 8.3.1.1 closes the route by which
new entrants are drawn toward a predator; this proposition closes the
route by which a predator's own search finds them. Both failing
simultaneously is what gives grid-wide predator starvation its full
force as new entrants avoid isolated predators by default while isolated
predators simultaneously lose the ability to find anyone at all.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.11c.3 (Mirror-Threat Suppression).}}

Independent of resource-based extraction incentive, define a suppression
factor on coupling likelihood between two high-entropy containers i and
j:

ζ\_mirror(i,j,t) = exp{[}−κ\_mirror · min(D\_op,i(t), D\_op,j(t)){]},
κ\_mirror \textgreater{} 0 fitted,

applied multiplicatively to whichever edge-formation quantity (Ξ, §8.3;
A\_first, §8.3.1; or a search-initiated contact under Definition
10.5b.11c.1) would otherwise govern the i-j pairing. Both parties must
register as entropic (both D\_op values contributing via the minimum)
for this suppression to activate meaningfully; a single entropic party
paired with a non-entropic one does not trigger it.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.11c.2 (Mirror-Threat Is Additive to, Not
Subsumed by, Resource Suppression).}}

§8.6a's X\_\{i,j\} already scales with target resources R\_j, so
extraction incentive between two severely resource-depleted high-entropy
containers is independently low without any mirror-threat mechanism.
Definition 10.5b.11c.3 does additional work precisely where resource
depletion does not already suppress coupling: two high-entropy
containers of differing severity, where the less-depleted party would
otherwise present exploitable resources to the more-depleted,
more-motivated party (§10.5b.18.25's dependent container searching under
Definition 10.5b.11c.1). Pure resource logic predicts this pairing
should occur at a nontrivial rate; ζ\_mirror(i,j,t) predicts it should
occur markedly less often than resource logic alone implies, because
recognizing another container's own entropic signature functions as a
threat signal --- the prospective target's own extractive capacity, not
only its resource level, is what a high-entropy searcher is registering
and avoiding.

\textbf{10.5b.12 Combined Intervention}

For both regimes, anchoring (a mechanical/behavioral intervention,
arresting oscillation and extraction) and active messaging change (an
informational intervention --- external correction, or the internal
rogue-metamessenger pathway of §10.5b.14) are more effective together
than either alone, whether applied pre-flip as prevention or post-flip
as recovery: anchoring alone can halt an oscillation without resolving
what caused it; messaging change alone, absent containment, risks
triggering F\_conf's destabilizing surge before it can take effect.
Formalized directly, rather than left as narrative alone: define each
intervention's own success probability independently, Anchor\_succ(t)
(halting oscillation, §10.5b.13) and Message\_succ(t) (correcting the
underlying divergence without triggering F\_conf, §10.5b.14). Applied
together rather than separately, real synergy exists specifically
because each intervention removes the condition that makes the other
risky:

Combined\_succ(t) = Anchor\_succ(t) + Message\_succ(t) −
Anchor\_succ(t)·Message\_succ(t) +
κ\_synergy·Anchor\_succ(t)·Message\_succ(t)·(1 −
Anchor\_succ(t)·Message\_succ(t)), κ\_synergy \textgreater{} 0 fitted,

where the first three terms are the ordinary probability of at least one
succeeding if independent, and the κ\_synergy term is the real,
additional gain specific to combination: anchoring removes messaging
change's own main risk (an uncontained F\_conf surge, since oscillation
is already arrested), and messaging change removes anchoring's own main
limitation (resolving root cause rather than only symptoms), so each
intervention's individual success probability is itself higher in
combination than its solo value used above --- κ\_synergy \textgreater{}
0 is the formal claim that this is genuine synergy, not merely two
independent chances stacked, and is directly falsifiable: observed
combined success rates exceeding the independence-only baseline (the
first three terms alone) support κ\_synergy \textgreater{} 0; rates
matching that baseline would falsify the synergy claim specifically,
without touching either intervention's own individual effectiveness.

\textbf{10.5b.13 Anchor Eligibility}

A low-entropy baseline container can safely anchor a destabilizing
neighbor, but only by actively maintaining zero extraction and zero
displacement flux for the full duration of the intervention --- i.e.,
holding X\_\{a,i\}(t) = 0 (§8.6a) throughout, a maintained behavioral
condition, not a structural guarantee. Lapsing on this vigilance risks
the anchor itself being drawn into an extraction cycle and reclassified
into the medium-entropy regime --- becoming a casualty rather than a
stabilizer.

A semi-rigid horizontal field --- a boundary configuration with no
exploitable structure to extract from or displace onto, per §2.3 --- is
structurally, not behaviorally, safe: X = 0 by construction, not by
maintained effort. This is the preferred anchor wherever available, but
is characteristically rare, particularly in human systems.

A third, structurally distinct type: a container or sub-structure
purpose-built or purpose-designated specifically to absorb another's
discharge --- a dedicated buffering structure maintained for this role,
rather than a general-purpose neighbor pressed into service. Unlike the
vigilant baseline container (whose safety is a maintained behavioral
condition) and the semi-rigid horizontal field (whose safety is
structural, requiring no maintained effort), a purpose-built anchor's
safety is a design property: it is safe to the extent it was actually
engineered for the load it absorbs, and its eligibility should be
assessed against its adapter-supplied designed capacity rather than
assumed. Exceeding that capacity converts it from an anchor into an
ordinary coupled neighbor, subject to the same risks as any other.

\emph{\textbf{Two distinct anchor-failure modes.}} Anchor eligibility as
established above answers whether an anchor is competent to absorb a
given discharge; it does not distinguish two genuinely different ways an
anchor can fail the container it is anchoring, which require different
correction:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Accidental wrong feedback.} An eligible, genuinely
  well-intentioned anchor whose own signal to the anchored container is
  simply miscalibrated --- wrong about what the container actually
  needs, without any exploitative intent. Correction is informational:
  the anchor's own feedback loop needs better data, not removal from the
  anchoring role.
\item
  \textbf{Deliberate predation.} An anchor whose signal is calibrated
  correctly for the anchor's own benefit rather than the anchored
  container's, exploiting the anchoring relationship's trust rather than
  failing to understand it. Correction requires removing the anchor from
  the role, since the failure is not informational.
\end{itemize}

Both are real, destructive failure modes producing similarly-damaged
outcomes for the anchored container, which is exactly why they need to
be told apart rather than merged into one ``bad anchor'' category:
applying the wrong correction (retraining a deliberately predatory
anchor, or removing an accidentally miscalibrated one who could have
been corrected) wastes the intervention.

\emph{\textbf{The dangerous-anchor typology.}} A further, real category
exists beyond both: an anchor that is genuinely, measurably effective at
anchoring some targets while being genuinely dangerous to others, where
the danger is not a failure of competence at all but a direct
consequence of the anchor's own underlying motivation. Define
Motive(a,t) as the adapter-supplied or inferred degree to which anchor
a's own participation is self-interested (deriving cover, power, or
standing from the anchoring role itself, rather than from the anchored
container's outcome):

Danger(a,j,t) = f(Motive(a,t), Vulnerability(j,t)), increasing in both,

where an anchor with high measured competence and high Motive(a,t) is
correctly eligible by the competence criterion above and simultaneously
dangerous to targets whose own vulnerability profile happens to serve
that motivation --- competence and danger are not opposites here, and
screening on competence alone, without also screening Motive(a,t),
systematically misses this category.

\textbf{10.5b.12b Counter-Momentum Availability}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.12b.1 (Counter-Momentum Availability).}}

For container i approaching a Flip Condition B event at time t, define:

\emph{Λ}\_counter(i,t) = Σ\_j Λ\_ij(t) · max(0,
−\textbf{g}\_C(i,t)·û\_ji(t)),

reusing the network coupling strength Λ\_ij (§8.5) and the structural
grain vector g\_C(t) already defined in §4.11.1, summed over
currently-coupled neighbors j whose own recent coupling direction toward
i opposes i's own dominant recent oscillation direction. This measures
the real, currently-available momentum-absorbing capacity in the network
immediately around i, not a hypothetical or historical one --- a
neighbor that has since decoupled or reversed its own orientation
contributes nothing.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.12b.1 (Backstop-Dependent Resolution).}}

Holding mass-weighting (μ\_o, μ\_p) fixed, a Flip Condition B event
resolves differently depending on Λ\_counter(i,t): where Λ\_counter(i,t)
exceeds a fitted threshold Λ\_backstop, the incoming discharge is
absorbed elastically against the positioned neighbor and the container
self-arrests (§6.9's bounded oscillation) rather than completing a
tipping outcome at all; where Λ\_counter(i,t) falls below that
threshold, the same discharge instead completes as a full 360°
reorganization (§10.5b.9's B2), since no real structure exists to return
the momentum to. This is a genuinely separate mechanism from
mass-weighting, not a restatement of it: a container can carry
mass-weighting favoring B2 and still self-arrest if a real backstop is
present, or carry mass-weighting favoring self-arrest and still complete
a 360° reorganization if no backstop is available at the moment of
tipping. Where an adapter cannot supply real, current coupling-direction
data for neighbors, Λ\_counter(i,t) defaults to 0 (no assumed backstop),
which is the conservative default consistent with this document's
general preference for not silently assuming protective structure that
has not been confirmed.

\hypertarget{b.13.1-mutual-anchor-clusters}{%
\subsection{10.5b.13.1 Mutual Anchor
Clusters}\label{b.13.1-mutual-anchor-clusters}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.13.1 (Mutual Anchor Cluster).}}

A set of containers \{C\_1,\ldots,C\_n\}, each in the low-entropy
baseline or medium-entropy regime, forms a mutual anchor cluster at time
t when their pairwise internal cohesion (Λ\_int over the set, §9.10.1)
exceeds a fitted threshold Λ\_mutual, and no individual member satisfies
any of §10.5b.13's three existing anchor-eligibility criteria on its
own. This is the defining structural difference from an ordinary anchor:
eligibility here is a property of the cluster's own coupling density,
not of any single member's behavioral vigilance, structural safety, or
designed capacity.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.13.2 (Reciprocal Support Density).}}

Within a mutual anchor cluster, §10.5b.18.9--18.11's external feedback
channel φ\_fb(i,j,t) and support flux Γ\_supp(i,j,t) --- defined
pairwise for an ordinary healthy-neighbor coupling --- are computed for
every ordered pair (i,j) within the cluster rather than in one
privileged direction. Define the cluster's reciprocal support density,

Φ\_mutual(t) = {[}Σ\_\{i,j ∈ cluster, i≠j\} Λ\_ij(t)·φ\_fb(i,j,t){]} /
{[}n(n−1){]},

the cluster-averaged bidirectional feedback rate, and analogously
Γ\_mutual(t) for support flux. Unlike a single anchor's one-directional
supply, every member of a mutual anchor cluster is simultaneously a
source and a recipient of both quantities --- Definition 10.5b.18.11's
correction forcing terms F\_ext,fb and F\_ext,supp apply to every
member's own D\_om(t) using the cluster's aggregate Φ\_mutual(t) and
Γ\_mutual(t) as their driving input, rather than each member needing a
distinct external anchor of its own.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.13.1 (Distributed Margin Benefit).}}

Each member's hull-breach margin (§16.3's margin\_i(t)) gains a positive
contribution from cluster membership,

margin\_i\^{}cluster(t) = margin\_i(t) + κ\_cluster ·
Φ\_mutual(t)·Γ\_mutual(t), κ\_cluster \textgreater{} 0 fitted,

distributing protective effect across the cluster rather than
concentrating it in one designated anchor's continued vigilance. This
has a direct consequence for cluster resilience under §8.8a's cascading
dynamics: where an ordinary anchor's failure or withdrawal removes
protection entirely (a single point of failure, consistent with
§10.5b.13's warning that a lapsing vigilant anchor becomes a casualty
itself), a mutual anchor cluster's protective effect degrades gradually
as Φ\_mutual(t) and Γ\_mutual(t) decline with membership loss, rather
than failing discontinuously when any single member is lost. A cluster
of size n loses protective capacity roughly in proportion to the
coupling density removed with a departing member, not catastrophically
as an ordinary anchor's failure would.

\hypertarget{b.13.2-permeability-flooding-in-high-entropy-containers-and-the-anchors-containment-role}{%
\subsection{10.5b.13.2 Permeability Flooding in High-Entropy Containers,
and the Anchor\textquotesingle s Containment
Role}\label{b.13.2-permeability-flooding-in-high-entropy-containers-and-the-anchors-containment-role}}

Definition 10.5b.13.2 (Real Causes of Permeability Flooding). Π\_f(i,t)
can rise past the firewall\textquotesingle s own maintained resistance
through several real, distinct pathways, none of which involve an
anchor: (a) Extraction/displacement threshold --- a sufficiently large
cumulative volume of extraction and displacement (Σ\_j X\_\{i,j\}, H\_i,
§8.6a), or a small number of particularly egregious acts, rather than a
single fixed trigger event. (b) Parasite-to-predator escalation --- a
shift in the container\textquotesingle s own extraction character from
sustained, sustainable parasitism to fast, destructive predation
(§8.6a.6\textquotesingle s degrees of parasitism), itself driving (c)
below faster than steady-state extraction would. (c) Resource-exhaustion
of the suppression apparatus, the shared mechanism underlying (a) and
(b): M\_decep\textquotesingle s own real cost (§10.5b.18.4) rises with
extraction severity and divergence, and unpaid M\_decep debt accrues
into δR (§7.9) exactly as any other resource debt does. Once that debt
exceeds what the container can continue paying, the firewall
(§10.5b.18.1) fails not because anything forced it, but because the
container can no longer afford to keep maintaining it --- chaos
previously confined to operational begins to permeate
official\textquotesingle s own understanding and sense of self as well,
not only its operational layer, a natural consequence of exhaustion
rather than an imposed event. (d) Decoupling-triggered external feedback
--- not a separately-invented phenomenon, but the specific,
already-formalized case of a Resolution Kick (§8.8b) landing on the
extracting container itself. Where a medium-entropy target j completes
its own tipping event while still coupled to its extractor i, K\_j(t)
reaches i carrying real, accurate signal about the extraction j actually
experienced (§8.8b.1) --- a violent transfer of accurate information,
not the vaguer ``violently decoupling target'' this cause was previously
described as without citing its actual mechanism. Whether this kick
produces flooding or is successfully absorbed is a real race condition:
define the patching rate Ṗ\_patch(i,t) as the rate at which
i\textquotesingle s operational-aligned metamessengers can rewrite or
resuppress incoming accurate signal via M\_o,t\^{}false (§10.5b.14.1),
and the kick\textquotesingle s own incoming rate as K\_j(t)/τ\_kick for
some real absorption window τ\_kick. Where Ṗ\_patch(i,t) ≥
K\_j(t)/τ\_kick, the container successfully re-suppresses the accurate
signal before it registers, and Π\_f(i,t) does not rise from this event;
where Ṗ\_patch(i,t) \textless{} K\_j(t)/τ\_kick, patching cannot keep
pace, and the excess feeds directly into (c)\textquotesingle s
resource-exhaustion mechanism, accelerating the container toward
flooding rather than causing it outright on its own.

None of (a) through (d) requires or involves an external anchor. An
anchor\textquotesingle s real function, defined here for the first time,
is containment of a flooding event these mechanisms have already
produced, not production of the flood itself: Contain(i,t) = 1 while an
eligible anchor (§10.5b.13) actively holds i stable --- absorbing
discharge, restricting extraction and displacement --- for the duration
of the instability (a)--(d) already caused, without itself contributing
to Π\_f(i,t)\textquotesingle s own rise.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.13.2.1 (Operational Death and Forced
Percolation).}} Once Π\_f\^{}forced(i,t) exceeds the firewall's
resistance for a sustained duration, the corrupted operational structure
the firewall was maintaining can no longer be sustained against the
now-unblocked feedback, and undergoes a genuine structural collapse
distinct from ordinary gradual correction --- the previous operational
organization ceases functioning before any replacement structure has
taken over, a real transitional state of zero organized structure, not a
smooth handoff. This transitional collapse necessarily produces a real,
elevated period of structural fragmentation and oscillation
(s\_frag(i,t) and σ\_p(i,t) both spike sharply, §10.5b.1), consistent
with and predicted directly by this mechanism rather than an incidental
side effect to be minimized. Only once this transitional collapse has
occurred does the container's own official-layer content --- established
at §10.5b.2.13 as genuinely accurate and intact throughout, merely cut
off from updating --- gain the open channel needed to flow downward into
the now-unoccupied operational layer, replacing the destroyed corrupted
structure with genuine negentropic organization rather than suppressing
the old structure's behavior while leaving it intact underneath.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.13.2.2 (Strength and Duration
Requirements).}} This mechanism requires anchoring substantially
stronger and longer than the medium-entropy case (§10.5b.13's ordinary
anchor eligibility), for three compounding, real reasons rather than
one: the anchor must first supply enough sustained A\_flood(t) to
overcome active firewall resistance, not merely pre-existing passive
inertia; it must remain stable and continue supplying that flooding
through the transitional collapse itself, precisely when the anchored
container is at its most volatile and most likely to discharge
instability onto anything nearby (§8.8a's cascading dynamics); and it
must sustain long enough for genuine percolation to complete, not merely
long enough for surface oscillation to subside, since the latter can
occur well before the former and would read externally as success while
the underlying corrupted structure remains intact. An anchor withdrawn
after oscillation subsides but before percolation completes should
expect relapse into the original corrupted configuration, since the
firewall's own resistance --- reduced but not structurally replaced ---
reasserts itself once the forcing lifts.

\hypertarget{b.14-rogue-metamessengers-and-slow-correction-reversal}{%
\subsection{10.5b.14 Rogue Metamessengers and Slow-Correction
Reversal}\label{b.14-rogue-metamessengers-and-slow-correction-reversal}}

A high-entropy container is not always healed only from outside. A
small, coordinated subset of a container's own meta-representation
apparatus --- aware the container's operational layer is corrupted and
actively working against what the container's official layer believes it
is doing --- can raise Π\_f(t) deliberately, provided the rate of
increase, not merely its eventual level, stays below whatever threshold
triggers F\_conf's destabilizing surge. Formally: a controlled injection
dΠ\_f/dt = ε\_rogue, with ε\_rogue small and bounded well under the rate
that would cross Π\_c abruptly. Held slow enough, this permits D\_op to
be metabolized incrementally through ordinary correction rather than
forcing a confrontation event, allowing gradual regime transition ---
high-entropy toward medium-entropy or, ideally, toward the low-entropy
baseline --- without ever triggering Flip Condition A.

\textbf{10.5b.14.1 Entropic, Negentropic, and Rogue Metamessengers}

Three terms, precisely distinguished, since two of them have been used
inconsistently in earlier drafts of this section.

\emph{\textbf{Negentropic metamessengers}} are, regime-generally and by
fixed meaning, those whose current orientation favors the container's
true, healthy signal --- the direction that reduces overall entropy if
it prevails.

\emph{\textbf{Entropic metamessengers}} are, regime-generally and by
fixed meaning, those whose orientation favors the container's false or
unhealthy signal --- the direction that sustains or increases overall
entropy if it prevails.

\emph{\textbf{Rogue metamessengers}} are defined relationally, not
absolutely: those whose current orientation opposes the operational
layer's own direction, whatever that direction currently is. Because
operational's own alignment differs by regime, ``rogue'' names a
different population in each:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{High-entropy:} operational is entropic. Rogues --- opposing
  operational --- are negentropic-aligned. Rogue and negentropic are the
  same population in this regime, and §10.5b.14's protective minority is
  properly named either way.
\item
  \textbf{Medium-entropy:} operational is negentropic. Rogues ---
  opposing operational --- are entropic-aligned. Rogue and entropic are
  the same population in this regime; negentropic metamessengers here
  are aligned \emph{with} operational and are explicitly not rogues.
  This is the population whose convergence with the official layer
  during a lateral knock predicts collapse rather than reset (§10.5b's
  convergence-direction account); a medium-entropy container whose
  metamessenger population is majority-rogue can never organize a large
  negentropic bulk at once, leaving its negentropic operational core
  persistently outweighed and the container correspondingly more
  vulnerable to knocks and to sustained coupling with predatory
  neighbors.
\end{itemize}

Formally, both entropic and negentropic fractions are already carried by
Ψ\_loyal(t) (§10.5b.18.12) within the closed population, and by
Ψ\_total(t) (§10.5b.18.14) across the whole population; ``rogue''
introduces no new quantity, only a regime-relational name for whichever
of these fractions currently opposes operational.

\textbf{10.5b.14.2 Emergency Firewall Reinforcement}

§10.5b.14's ε\_rogue mechanism describes deliberate, slow, controlled
disclosure --- a routine, ongoing negentropic action. A separate, faster
mechanism exists for a different situation entirely: an acute, large
spike in Π\_f(t), of a magnitude routine leak-patching (§10.5b.18.1's
M\_o,t\^{}false, event-triggered around ordinary glitches) is not
equipped to contain, and which risks catastrophic, uncontrolled feedback
flooding if left unaddressed.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.14.2 (Emergency Firewall
Reinforcement).}}

Where Π\_f(t) crosses a fitted large-spike threshold Θ\_spike --- set
well above the routine fluctuation range already characteristic of the
high-entropy regime, distinguishing a genuine flooding risk from an
ordinary small leak --- the negentropic-aligned (rogue) fraction of the
closed population, Ψ\_loyal(t), executes an automatic, emergency
response: official's memory is rapidly restored toward its pre-spike
baseline, and an additional restorative pull is applied directly to
Π\_f(t) itself,

F\_emerg(t) = κ\_emerg · Ψ\_loyal(t) · (Π\_f(t) − Θ\_spike)₊, κ\_emerg
\textgreater{} 0 fitted,

pulling permeability back below the spike threshold. This is
mechanically distinct from routine cover-maintenance despite
superficially resembling it: routine patching (entropic-aligned,
triggered by any leak, purpose is sustaining cover-and-lure) and
emergency reinforcement (negentropic-aligned, triggered only by a large
spike, purpose is preventing collapse) can produce a similarly-shaped
memory correction while being executed by opposite populations for
opposite reasons. A high enough, sustained Π\_f(t) that F\_emerg(t)
cannot bring back under Θ\_spike proceeds toward Flip Condition A's
ordinary tipping machinery (§10.5b.7--§10.5b.9) rather than being
contained.

\emph{\textbf{The rogue metamessenger tradeoff, stated explicitly.}}
§10.5b.14's ε\_rogue mechanism accrues real self-deception debt
(M\_decep, §10.5b.18.4) as a side effect of slow, controlled disclosure,
and §10.5b.14.2's emergency reinforcement accrues comparable cost
restoring memory toward baseline. Neither mechanism is cost-free, and
both are chosen anyway because the rogue-negentropic population judges
the accrued debt the lesser of two evils against the alternative:
uncontrolled feedback flooding and the collapse it would otherwise
cause. This is a deliberate, known tradeoff internal to the mechanism's
own operation, not an unintended side effect discovered after the fact
--- the debt is accepted, not merely incurred.

\hypertarget{b.14.3-systems-override-high-entropy-and-medium-entropy-versions}{%
\subsection{10.5b.14.3 Systems Override, High-Entropy and Medium-Entropy
Versions}\label{b.14.3-systems-override-high-entropy-and-medium-entropy-versions}}

Definition 10.5b.14.3a (High-Entropy Systems Override). Operational
already holds default control (𝒜(t) = 1, §10.5b.18.18) in the
high-entropy regime. Override here is operational actively reinforcing
or exercising that control specifically in response to a real threat
signal (Θ\_threat(i,j,t) \textgreater{} 0, §8.6a.9) --- either by
resisting any ceding of 𝒜(t) it would otherwise allow, or by directing
operational-aligned (entropic) metamessengers to accelerate the rate of
M\_o,t\^{}false substitution (§10.5b.14.1) specifically to keep the
threatening signal from reaching official at all. This is not a new
primitive: it is a real, threat-triggered intensification of
control-locus assertion and reality-rewriting rate, both already
defined, occurring together specifically because
operational\textquotesingle s own survival is what the threat endangers.
It is control-shaped by Definition 10.5b.0.11\textquotesingle s own
criteria, and remains harmful under the Do No Harm Principle
(§10.5b.0.11) except where operational is itself acting to reverse an
imminent terminal lock, the one exception that principle already carves
out. This is not limited to threat response: the same override triggers
during active predation itself --- while operational is administering
extraction and displacement knocks (§8.6a) against a target --- not only
when a threat signal is present. In the predation case, the
override\textquotesingle s distinctive further effect is retrospective:
official receives a different version of events, or a constructed
justification for them, after the fact --- not merely a suppressed or
absent signal, but an actively fabricated account of what operational
did and why. This retrospective narrative construction, distinct from
the real-time M\_o,t\^{}false substitution already covered, is what
makes this a genuinely dangerous mechanism rather than a passive
information gap: official is not left uninformed, it is actively
misinformed about its own container\textquotesingle s specific past
actions.

Definition 10.5b.14.3b (Medium-Entropy Systems Override). A structurally
opposite mechanism: medium-entropy\textquotesingle s operational layer
is genuinely intact and negentropic (§10.5b.2.13\textquotesingle s Type
B), and override here is that intact operational layer sending a real,
discrete intervention --- an instruction or corrective signal into the
container\textquotesingle s own dynamics --- timed one of two ways.
Pre-emptive: triggered while M\_tip(t) is still below the hull-breach
threshold (§10.5a), intervening early enough to prevent the breach from
occurring at all. Mid-collapse: triggered after breach has already
occurred, while the container is still moving through a Flip B
trajectory (§10.5b.7--10.5b.9) toward an outcome not yet resolved,
steering that resolution toward B2 (full reorganization, the
medium-entropy 360 reset) rather than B1 (terminal lock) --- riding the
collapse already underway through to reset rather than preventing it.
Both are the same mechanism at different timing, not two separate ones:
operational\textquotesingle s own genuine capacity to act, exercised
either before the threshold is crossed or after it, in service of the
same outcome. The real, characteristic outcome of this mechanism,
distinct from merely reaching B2 over an unspecified trajectory: it
frequently saves the container from collapsing at all, improves feedback
permeability in the healthy sense --- rising Π\_f(t) here reflects
genuine opening, not the resource-exhaustion failure of §10.5b.13.2 ---
vents accumulated divergence pressure in the process, and moves the
container toward the low-entropy baseline rather than merely toward a
labeled tipping outcome. This is the concrete, positive content behind
calling it a healthy mechanism: real resolution of accumulated pressure
and real movement toward baseline, not just avoidance of the worse
alternative.

Override\_high(i,t) = 1 when Θ\_threat(i,j,t) \textgreater{} 0 triggers
reinforced 𝒜(t) retention or accelerated M\_o,t\^{}false substitution
rate, per Definition 10.5b.14.3a. Override\_medium(i,t) = 1 when
operational\textquotesingle s own intervention is triggered either
pre-breach (shifting M\_tip\textquotesingle s own trajectory below
threshold) or mid-collapse (shifting the B1/B2 mass-weighting split,
§10.5b.7, toward B2), per Definition 10.5b.14.3b. Neither version is
performed by metamessengers of either loyalty; Ψ\_loyal(t),
Ψ\_loyal\^{}corrob(t), and the rogue-metamessenger mechanisms above
(§10.5b.14, §10.5b.14.1--10.5b.14.2) remain entirely distinct processes,
operating on permeability and disclosure rather than on control-locus
assertion or direct trajectory intervention.

\textbf{10.5b.15 Two Collapse-Layer Axioms}

The mechanisms above rest on two claims general enough, and load-bearing
enough, to state as axioms of the collapse layer specifically, alongside
Axioms C3 and C4 (§10.5b.0) governing the same layer's
structural-geometric side. C1 and C2 below govern divergence dynamics;
C3 and C4 govern structural geometry; all four are distinct from Part
3's foundational axioms.

\emph{\textbf{Axiom C1 (Truth-Only Reorganization).}}

The feedback operator ℱ (§6.4) admits stable, unforced fixed points only
at convergence toward the container's true underlying signal.
Convergence toward a false signal is not a fixed point ℱ can reach or
sustain on its own; it requires continuous external or self-generated
forcing (F\_conf, or an extractive input per §8.6a) to be maintained,
and decays toward the true-signal fixed point once that forcing is
withdrawn --- formalized directly by §6.4's landscape Φ reshaping once
artificial forcing that was locally propping open a false-signal valley
is removed.

\emph{\textbf{Epistemic status of ``true underlying signal.''}} This is
an ontological quantity within the theory --- what the axiom asserts ℱ's
unforced fixed points converge toward --- not something automatically
identified with whatever the model currently estimates. Its empirical
identification requires an admissible grounding procedure (G(t), §6.3.2)
and may itself remain latent or only estimated, subject to the same
identifiability limits already stated there: an estimate agreed upon by
several apparently independent parties is not thereby confirmed to be
the true signal, only agreed-upon. Stating this explicitly here
forecloses a reading in which the theory smuggles its own desired answer
into the landscape by silently treating ``the model's current best
estimate'' and ``the true underlying signal'' as the same thing.

\emph{\textbf{Axiom C2 (Runaway Acceleration).}}

As a container approaches a hull-breach crossing (§10.5a) while
remaining in Flip Condition A, its instantaneous divergence pressure
accelerates rather than approaching the threshold at constant or
decelerating rate. This is stated as a falsifiable claim: it predicts
that d(D\_op)/dt and dS/dt should show a measurable, characteristic
upward inflection within a fitted pre-breach window {[}t\_breach −
w\_C2, t\_breach{]}, w\_C2 fitted or domain-defined, distinguishable
from a null model in which divergence approaches the threshold as an
undifferentiated random walk. Fixing w\_C2 in advance, rather than
choosing it after inspecting the data, is what keeps this a genuine
prediction rather than a pattern any analyst could locate
retrospectively in a sufficiently flexible window.

\emph{\textbf{Corollary C2.1 (Continued Oscillation Through Collapse).}}
Axiom C2's acceleration is not a claim that oscillation itself ceases
once the threshold region is entered --- a real, separate claim this
document had left implicit. A container past the point Axiom C2
describes continues to oscillate, per §10.5b.9's own coherence and
momentum dynamics, for the full remaining duration of its approach to
and passage through terminal lock: σ\_p(t) and σ\_o(t) (§10.5b.1's
trailing-window variances) should remain elevated and measurably nonzero
throughout the pre-breach window, not decay toward a smooth,
non-oscillating trajectory as the container is propelled toward
collapse. The propulsion Axiom C2 describes and the oscillation §10.5b.1
already tracks are not alternatives --- a collapsing container is
propelled by its own accumulating momentum while continuing to waver,
the way a falling object continues vibrating from its own structural
stress on the way down rather than falling in smooth, rigid silence. An
adapter observing oscillation decay to near-zero immediately before a
confirmed hull breach should treat that pattern as evidence against
ordinary Flip Condition A dynamics, not as confirmation of them.

\emph{\textbf{Axiom C5 (Bidirectional Closure --- the Horseshoe
Principle).}}

A container's feedback operator ℱ (§6.4) tracks truth only when its rate
of adaptive updating is genuinely responsive to real divergence pressure
--- moving more when divergence is high, settling when divergence is
low. A container whose rate of updating is decoupled from divergence
pressure is in a closed configuration in the sense of Axiom C1,
regardless of which direction that decoupling runs: a container pinned
near zero velocity regardless of divergence (rigid closure) and a
container pinned near maximal velocity regardless of divergence
(compulsive closure) are the same failure --- both require continuous
external or self-generated forcing to sustain, and both decay toward
genuine, divergence-responsive tracking once that forcing is withdrawn.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.15.1 (Tracking Fidelity).}}

Over a trailing window of length w, allowing for realistic response lag
δ ∈ {[}0, δ\_max{]} rather than requiring simultaneity:

\emph{Φ}\_track(t) = max\_\{δ∈{[}0,δ\_max{]}\} corr\_\{s∈{[}t−w,t{]}\}(
‖v(s−δ)‖\_g , D(s) ).

Both horseshoe extremes are diagnosed by the same underlying signature
--- a mismatch between what a container's divergence pressure D(t) is
doing and what its velocity is doing --- rather than by simply flagging
low variation in movement:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Rigid closure: D(t) elevated or rising while ‖v(t)‖\_g stays pinned
  \textgreater{} \textgreater{} near its floor across the window --- the
  container is not \textgreater{} moving \textgreater{} despite a real,
  worsening problem.
\item
  Compulsive closure: ‖v(t)‖\_g stays pinned near its ceiling while
  \textgreater{} \textgreater{} D(t) is low or falling across the window
  --- the container keeps \textgreater{} \textgreater{} moving despite
  having no real problem left to move about.
\item
  Neither is triggered when D(t) and ‖v(t)‖\_g are jointly low ---
  \textgreater{} \textgreater{} genuine, healthy equilibrium (§6.9), not
  a diagnostic failure. \textgreater{} \textgreater{} Note that a
  high-entropy container's velocity already \textgreater{} reflects
  \textgreater{} its full internal state, including its churning
  \textgreater{} operational \textgreater{} layer (§4.3); such a
  container is not quiet in this \textgreater{} sense even
  \textgreater{} though its official layer alone would appear calm, so
  \textgreater{} this \textgreater{} diagnostic does not misfire on it
  --- the apparent-vs-actual \textgreater{} \textgreater{} distinction
  is exactly what §10.5b.1's σ\_o/σ\_p classification \textgreater{}
  \textgreater{} already tracks separately.
\end{itemize}

The theory does not need a separate diagnostic for each extreme; the
same test that fails to find a real relationship between motion and
cause catches both. §16.10's Definition 16.10 (Structural Baseline)
supplies the comparison baseline this diagnostic needs before ``pinned
near its floor/ceiling'' can be applied to a real case, since these are
meaningful only relative to a container's own typical range.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.15.2 (Instantaneous Velocity Crossing).}}

Any oscillating trajectory, however violent, passes through ‖v(t)‖\_g ≈
0 naturally each time it reverses direction. Define the instantaneous
crossing set 𝒵(j) = \{t : ‖v\_j(t)‖\_g \textless{} ε\_zero\} for a
fitted tolerance ε\_zero, distinct from Φ\_track(j,t) (§10.5b.15.1),
which is computed over a full trailing window w and cannot be satisfied
by a single crossing alone.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.15.1 (Sampling Fidelity Requirement).}}

A container i's coupling or engagement decision toward a neighbor j
(§8.3's edge existence, §10.5b.11a-c's targeting and search mechanics)
should be gated on Φ\_track(j,t) --- the same windowed diagnostic
§10.5b.15.1 already uses for self-diagnosis --- rather than on a raw,
single-timepoint observation of ‖v\_j(t)‖\_g. Where i's own observation
of j happens to fall within 𝒵(j) and i treats that single sample as
evidence of §6.9's genuine equilibrium, i has committed a real,
falsifiable misread: engaging with j on the belief that j has reached
stability, when j's own windowed Φ\_track would show continued high
oscillation. This is a real, distinct failure mode from the existing
apparent-vs-actual warning (§10.5b.1, §15.2), which concerns a
container's \emph{official layer} misrepresenting its own state; this
failure occurs even when j's official and operational layers are both
being observed accurately, purely because the observing container i
sampled at the wrong instant. An adapter supporting inter-container
coupling decisions should therefore expose Φ\_track(j,t) as an
observable neighbors can query, not merely ‖v\_j(t)‖\_g at the current
instant.

\hypertarget{b.15.3-epistemic-capture}{%
\subsection{10.5b.15.3 Epistemic
Capture}\label{b.15.3-epistemic-capture}}

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.15.3 (Epistemic Capture).}} For a
medium-entropy container i, define capture as a real, near-total
collapse of feedback permeability specifically toward alternative
framings rather than toward correction in general:

EC(i,t) = 1 − Π\_f\^{}alt(i,t)/Π\_f\^{}ref,

where Π\_f\^{}alt(i,t) is i's feedback permeability specifically to
signals contradicting its currently-dominant entropic self-narrative (a
real, narrower quantity than ordinary Π\_f, §6.5, which does not
distinguish confirming from disconfirming signal), and Π\_f\^{}ref is a
domain-specified reference level for genuinely open alternative-signal
reception. EC(i,t) → 1 is the formal content of the entropic message no
longer being received as one signal among others, but as the whole of
perceived reality --- distinct from, and more severe than, ordinary low
permeability, which still admits the theoretical possibility of
alternative signal even when little arrives.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.15.3.1 (Capture Raises the Real Cost of
Rescue).}} Where EC(i,t) is high, ordinary anchoring and messaging
interventions (§10.5b.13's anchor eligibility, §10.5b.18.9's support
functions) require a real, additional penalty to reach i at all, since
the intervention itself is exactly the kind of alternative-framing
signal EC(i,t) is defined to suppress:

effective intervention strength = raw intervention strength · (1 −
EC(i,t)),

recovering ordinary, undiscounted intervention effectiveness when
EC(i,t) → 0, and correctly predicting that the same anchoring effort
which reliably helps a merely-drawn-toward-the-sink container can fail
outright against a genuinely captured one --- a real, falsifiable
distinction this document's existing machinery, treating extraction pull
as the only relevant medium-entropy vulnerability, could not previously
draw.

\hypertarget{b.16-compulsive-closure-and-the-boomerang-mechanism}{%
\subsection{10.5b.16 Compulsive Closure and the Boomerang
Mechanism}\label{b.16-compulsive-closure-and-the-boomerang-mechanism}}

A container may become closed not through rigidity but through its
opposite: a fixed commitment to producing change, sufficient that
reaching genuine alignment removes the compulsion's reason for existing,
and the compulsion --- rather than subsiding --- manufactures fresh
divergence to resolve.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.16.1 (Alignment-Averse Forcing).}}

For a container diagnosed with compulsive closure (Definition
10.5b.15.1: Φ\_track degenerate with v pinned high), define a transient
forcing term active only as the container's aggregate divergence
approaches its own historical floor:

\emph{F}\_boom(t) = κ\_boom · Π\_f(t) · 𝟙{[}D(t) \textless{} D\_low{]} ·
(D\_low − D(t)), κ\_boom \textgreater{} 0 fitted.

D\_low is a fitted proximity-to-alignment threshold, analogous in role
to §10.5b.3's Π\_c.~F\_boom(t) enters §6.4's landscape dynamics as a
term opposing the ordinary downhill drift, pushing the container away
from Φ's minimum rather than toward it. Its magnitude is largest exactly
at D(t) → 0 and fades as the container moves back into divergence ---
the mirror image of F\_conf(t) (§10.5b.3), which is largest under
elevated divergence and fades toward baseline. Where F\_conf formalizes
a struggling container's pain at confronting its own bad numbers,
F\_boom formalizes a compulsively-closed container's inability to
tolerate having no bad numbers left to confront.

\hypertarget{b.17-binary-trust-sorting-and-the-inversion-failure}{%
\subsection{10.5b.17 Binary Trust Sorting and the Inversion
Failure}\label{b.17-binary-trust-sorting-and-the-inversion-failure}}

A container attempting to direct help toward its own struggling members
must correctly identify which members are net extractors and which are
net victims (§8.6a). Where a container has previously adopted a binary
trust classification of its own neighbors --- sorting them into a
trusted set and a suspect set, rather than continuously tracking their
actual extraction behavior --- that classification can become a
liability distinct from ordinary misdiagnosis.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.17.1 (Binary Trust Label).}}

A container C possessing this precondition maintains a label ℓ :
\{C\_j\} → \{trusted, suspect\} over its neighbors, updated on a slower
timescale than the underlying extraction dynamics ρ(j), X\_\{i,j\}
(§8.6a) --- the defining feature of the precondition is that ℓ is sticky
relative to the dynamics it is meant to track, not that it is wrong at
any single instant.

\emph{\textbf{Definition 10.5b.17.2 (Inversion Condition).}}

Once C's own divergence pressure ((1−I(t)) + S̃(t), §10.5a) exceeds a
fitted confusion threshold, ℓ's assignment inverts relative to actual
role: neighbors with high outbound extraction flux (§8.6a's X\_\{j,k\})
are disproportionately likely to hold the trusted label, while neighbors
who are net victims --- visibly distressed, elevated σ\_o or high
inbound extraction --- are disproportionately likely to hold the suspect
label. The mechanism is not random misclassification; it is that visible
distress is read as evidence for the suspect label rather than as
evidence of victimhood, precisely when C is least able to tell the
difference.

\emph{\textbf{Proposition 10.5b.17.1 (Misdirected Anchoring Expands
Reach).}}

Where C extends anchoring or resource support (§10.5b.13) preferentially
to trusted-labeled neighbors under the inversion condition, and the
